Tautological systems, homogeneous spaces and the holonomic rank problem
Paul Görlach, Thomas Reichelt, Christian Sevenheck, Avi Steiner, Uli Walther

Abstract
Many hypergeometric differential systems that arise from a geometric setting can be endowed with the structure of mixed Hodge modules. We generalize this fundamental result to the tautological systems associated to homogeneous spaces by giving a functorial construction for them. As an application, we solve the holonomic rank problem for such tautological systems in full generality.

†
†

Keywords: Tautological system, Fourier–Laplace transformation, mixed Hodge module, Lie group, homogeneous space, representation

1
Introduction

The purpose of this paper is to investigate differential systems that one can naturally associate to group actions on smooth algebraic varieties, and more specifically to representations of algebraic groups. A well-known and widely studied case is when the group is an algebraic torus, in which case the corresponding
\mathscr{D}
-modules are known as GKZ-systems (see, e.g.,
[
RSSW21
]
for an overview on the algebraic aspects of this theory). Consider now the following
data: a complex algebraic group
acting linearly on a finite-dimensional vector space, an invariant subvariety of this space, and a homomorphism from the Lie algebra of the group into the complex numbers. To this situation is naturally attached a
tautological system
, which is an equivariant
\mathscr{D}
-module on the dual vector space. This construction seems to go back to
[
Hot98
, Section 4.]
, but has been considered more recently in a series of papers by Bloch, Huang, Lian, Song, Yau and Zhu (
[
LSY13
,
BHL+14
,
LY13
,
HLZ16
]
). One of their main motivations comes from mirror symmetry, understood in the classical sense of recovering enumerative geometry information (i.e., quantum cohomology) of certain symplectic varieties by period integral computations of their mirror families (or oscillating integrals in the non-Calabi–Yau case). While the case of complete intersections inside toric varieties can be considered as settled (at least under sufficient positivity assumptions, cf.
[
Giv98
,
Iri11
,
RS17
]
and the respective bibliography trees), it is a longstanding and challenging problem to establish mirror symmetry, expressed as an equivalence of
\mathscr{D}
-modules (possibly with additional structures, such as Hodge modules or irregular variants of them) for non-toric varieties. An important class of examples arise from homogeneous spaces; for a partial list of known results on mirror symmetry in that context see
[
Rie08
,
MR20
,
LT17
]
. A common feature of these papers is that the mirror of a Fano manifold which is a homogeneous space for some group

G^{\vee}
consists of a Landau–Ginzburg potential, constructed via Lie theoretic methods from the Langlands dual group
G
of
G^{\vee}
. When restricted to a torus inside
G
, such a potential function can be expressed as a Laurent polynomial. Describing, and then studying, an appropriate partial compactification of this mirror Laurent polynomial
is a major and central problem in the area;
the toric situation is considered for example in
[
RS17
]
and, from a very different point of view,
in
[
CPS22
]
.

Motivated by these results and problems, a question of fundamental importance is, for a given homogeneous space
X=G/P
, to describe the differential system satisfied by periods of families of hyperplane sections, for an appropriate embedding of
X
into a projective space. Such a differential system would be the analogue of a GKZ-
\mathscr{D}
-module, and should yield (by dimensional reduction) the mirror
\mathscr{D}
-module considered in the papers mentioned above.
Our main findings paraphrased in
1.2
below, give criteria to determine when tautological systems arise as such
\mathscr{D}
-modules in a setting where we allow
G
to be any linear algebraic group, and where the representation will be in the space of sections of some equivariant line bundle
L
on
X
.

Our investigations show that one needs to impose rather delicate conditions on the bundle
L
and the parameter Lie algebra homomorphism mentioned above in order to obtain a non-zero tautological system. If these conditions—which we make explicit—hold true, we show that the corresponding tautological system has a functorial description, and thus naturally underlies a mixed Hodge module. We determine its possible weights, and we show how to compute its solution rank
at any point. In particular, we determine its holonomic rank in terms of the dimension of the cohomology of a natural family of (complements of) hyperplane sections of
X
. The latter result gives a complete solution for arbitrary line bundles to the holonomic rank problem raised in
[
BHL+14
]
. We also show that in many cases (depending on the value of the parameter Lie algebra homomorphism), the monodromy representation defined by the smooth part of the tautological system is irreducible.
Besides applications to mirror symmetry, our results should also lay the foundations for further study of Hodge theory of various differential modules constructed from representations of algebraic groups, such as Frenkel–Gross connections (see
[
FG09
]
) or generalized Kloosterman
\mathscr{D}
-modules (
[
HNY13
]
).

In the remainder of this introduction, we are going to describe our main results in more detail, and we give an overview on the content of this paper. The main character, the tautological system, is defined below. In terms of notation, for a vector space
V
and its dual space
W:=V^{\vee}
, we denote the Fourier–Laplace transformation functor

\FL^{V}\colon\textup{Mod}(\mathscr{D}_{V})\rightarrow\textup{Mod}(\mathscr{D}_{W})
(see
Section

3.1
below for more details about Fourier–Laplace transformations on arbitrary vector bundles). For now,
G^{\prime}
can be any linear algebraic group, but in the
later parts we will consider a group
G
acting transitively on a variety
X
, and
G^{\prime}
will denote the
product
{\mathds{C}}^{*}\times G
, acting on equivariant line bundles
L\rightarrow X
.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
\rho\colon G^{\prime}\to\GL(V)
be a finite-dimensional rational representation of an algebraic group and denote the induced Lie algebra representation by
\differential\rho\colon\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{gl}(V)
. Let
\overline{Y}
be a
G^{\prime}
-invariant closed subvariety of
V
. For a Lie algebra homomorphism
\beta\colon\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\to\mathds{C}
, define the left
\mathscr{D}_{V}
-module

\hat{\tau}(\rho,\overline{Y},\beta):=\mathscr{D}_{V}/\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathscr{D}_{V}\mathcal{I}+\mathscr{D}_{V}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{V}(\xi)-\beta^{\prime}(\xi)\mid\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}}\right)}}\right),

(1)

where
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{V}
is the vanishing ideal of
\overline{Y}
, where

Z_{V}(\xi)
denotes the vector field on
V
given by the infinitesimal action of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
(see
4.3
for a detailed discussion), and where
\beta^{\prime}(\xi):=\trace(\differential\rho(\xi))-\beta(\xi)
.

Its Fourier–Laplace transform

\tau(\rho,\overline{Y},\beta):=\FL^{V}(\hat{\tau}(\rho,\overline{Y},\beta))

(2)

is a left
\mathscr{D}_{V^{\vee}}
-module called the
tautological system
associated to
\rho
,
\overline{Y}
and
\beta
.

The next statement summarizes our main results.
To state them, assume that
X
is a smooth projective variety, and that
G
is a reductive and connected linear algebraic group that acts transitively on
X
. Suppose that
L\rightarrow X
is a
G
-equivariant line bundle on
X
, with sheaf of sections
\mathscr{L}
,
which we assume to be very ample. We put
G^{\prime}:={\mathds{C}}^{*}\times G
, and we define an action of
G^{\prime}
on
L
by letting the
{\mathds{C}}^{*}
-factor act via inverse scaling in the fibres of
L
(see
4.22
for a more precise and more general description). Setting
V:=H^{0}(X,\mathscr{L})^{\vee}
, we obtain a representation
G^{\prime}\rightarrow\GL(V)
. Moreover, since
L
is very ample, the linear system
|\mathscr{L}|
yields an embedding
g\colon X\hookrightarrow\mathds{P}V
. Let
\hat{X}\subseteq V
be the affine cone; this is a
G^{\prime}
-invariant subvariety.
Notice that there is an isomorphism
L^{*}\cong\hat{X}\setminus\{0\}
, where
L^{*}
is the complement of the zero section of
L\rightarrow X

and we write

\iota\colon L^{*}\hookrightarrow V
for the corresponding locally closed embedding obtained by composing this isomorphism with the embedding
\hat{X}\setminus\{0\}\hookrightarrow V
.
Choose any Lie algebra homomorphism
\beta\colon\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}={\mathds{C}}\mathbf{e}\oplus\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow{\mathds{C}}
with
\beta_{|\mathfrak{g}}=0
(this is forced on
\beta
if
G
is semisimple, since then
[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]=\mathfrak{g}
), i.e., choose a number
\beta(\mathbf{e})\in{\mathds{C}}
.

Theorem 1.2

(
4.34
,
6.14
and
6.15
)
.

In the above situation, the following statements hold true.

1.

Let
\beta(\mathbf{e})\notin{\mathds{Z}}
. We have

(a)

\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\FL^{V}(\iota_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k})&&\textup{if }\mathscr{L}^{\otimes\ell}\cong\omega^{\otimes(-k)}_{X}\textup{ and }\beta(\mathbf{e})=\ell/k,\\
\\
0&&\textup{else},\end{array}}}\right.

where
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k}
is a smooth
\mathscr{D}_{L^{*}}
-module of rank
1
on
L^{*}
(and we denote by
\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k}

its associated local system) which underlies a pure complex Hodge module of weight
\dim(X)+1
.

(b)

If
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\neq 0
, then it underlies a simple pure complex Hodge module of weight
\dim(X)+\dim(V^{\vee})
. In particular, the local system corresponding to the restriction of
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
to the complement of its singular locus (or, phrased differently, its monodromy representation) is irreducible.

(c)

The holonomic rank of
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
equals

\dim_{\mathds{C}}H^{\dim(X)}_{c}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X\setminus Z(\lambda),\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{\lambda}^{\ell/k}}}\right)

for a generic
\lambda\in V^{\vee}=H^{0}(X,\mathscr{L})
, where

Z(\lambda)
is the vanishing locus in
X
of the section
\lambda
, and where

\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{\lambda}^{\ell/k}
is the local system

\lambda_{|X\setminus Z(\lambda)}^{*}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k}
.

2.

Let
\beta(\mathbf{e})\in{\mathds{Z}}_{>0}
. We then have

\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)=\FL^{V}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(H^{0}\iota_{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}}}\right),

which underlies a (rational) mixed Hodge module (i.e. an element in
\textup{MHM}(V^{\vee})
), with weights in
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\dim(X)+\dim(V^{\vee}),\,\dim(X)+\dim(V^{\vee})+1}}\right\}
. Its holonomic rank is given by

\dim_{\mathds{C}}H^{\dim(X)}(X\setminus Z(\lambda),{\mathds{C}}).

Since the above theorem is meant only as an overview of our results, we ignore the case where
\beta(\mathbf{e})\in{\mathds{Z}}_{\leq 0}
here, as it is essentially uninteresting (see
6.13
for more details).
In a similar spirit, we only mention the holonomic rank here, whereas

6.15
contains finer results concering the fibre rank (resp. the solution rank) of the system
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
at any point. Notice further that the points 1.(a) and 1.(b) in the above theorem imply in particular that for any given equivariant line bundle
L
that gives a non-zero tautological system, some sufficiently high power of it yields a system with irreducible monodromy representation.

There are essentially three main ingredients in the proof of the above results. First, one needs to rewrite the
Fourier–Laplace transformation entering in the definition of the tautological system in (
2
) as an operation that involves only functors defined in the category of mixed Hodge modules. This is done using a strategy that already appeared in
[
Rei14
]
, namely, via the Radon transformation for algebraic
\mathscr{D}_{{\mathds{P}}^{n}}
-modules.
Due to the possible non-integrality of
\beta
however, we need here a variant of this transformation.
This twisted Radon transformation was used in
[
RS20
, Section 5.2]
, and the relevant adaptations are discussed in
Section

3
.

The second main point of our investigations is to study non-vanishing criteria for tautological systems. As already mentioned, a tautological system, as defined by (
2
) resp. (
1
) is often the zero module, especially when the dimension of the group
G
is larger than the dimension of the
G
-variety
Y
. This is exactly the situation that we are facing when studying tautological systems defined by homogeneous spaces. This aspect seems to have been overlooked in the previous studies of tautological systems (e.g. in
[
BHL+14
,
HLZ16
]
).
We therefore need to develop both necessary and sufficient criteria for such systems to be non-zero. As stated in our main result above, they involve both constraints on the equivariant line bundle
L
and on the parameter homomorphism
\beta
. We develop these criteria in
Sections

4
and
5
, using some facts on modules over a rings of twisted differential operators as well as some standard techniques from representation theory.

The third important ingredient of our construction is a localization result for the Fourier–Laplace transform
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
of the tautological system, treated in
Section

6.1
and
Section

6.2
. Although the problem is similar to the corresponding result in the GKZ-case in
[
SW09
]
, the techniques are very much different. It is here where the two cases

\beta(\mathbf{e})\in{\mathds{Z}}
resp.
\beta(\mathbf{e})\notin{\mathds{Z}}

need to be treated separately. While the latter is a relatively simple argument concerning eigenvalue decomposition for an operator derived from the Euler vector field on the space
V
, the former is more delicate. Contrary to the strategy in
[
SW09
]
(using so-called Euler-Koszul homology) we study here various Lie algebroid cohomologies and prove some vanishing theorems about them.

Outline:

Let us give a more specific overview over the various parts of the paper. Notice that the level of generality is decreasing, in the sense that the results in the earlier sections apply to more general situations than the main result as stated above. In particular, Section
4

contains results of general interest about

\mathscr{D}
-modules related to group actions on algebraic varieties.

We start by defining in
Section

2
certain Hodge modules on
line bundles
L\rightarrow X
over smooth varieties
(or rather on the complement of the zero section
L^{*}
). Their underlying
\mathscr{D}
-modules (denoted by
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}
) generalize the twisted structure sheaf
\mathscr{D}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}/\mathscr{D}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}(\partial_{t}\cdot t+\beta)

(which would correspond to the case where the variety is a point).
Then we study their Fourier–Laplace transforms in
Section

3
, show that they still underly a mixed Hodge module on the dual bundle and discuss a (complex of)
\mathscr{D}
-module(s) on the space of global sections of this dual bundle as well as a geometric interpretation of it as twisted cohomology of hyperplane sections.

In
Section

4
we address the question under which hypotheses the tautological system

\tau(\rho,\overline{Y},\beta)
and its Fourier transform
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\overline{Y},\beta)
are a non-zero
\mathscr{D}_{V^{\vee}}
- resp.
\mathscr{D}_{V}
-module. We first consider a quite general situation of a smooth algebraic variety
Y
endowed with the action of an algebraic group
G^{\prime}
. From the vector fields induced by this group action, together with a Lie algebra homomorphism
\beta
, we construct a
\mathscr{D}_{Y}
-module
\mathscr{N}_{Y}^{\beta}
. Especially important is the case where
Y
occurs as an orbit in a vector space
V
underlying a rational representation
\rho\colon G^{\prime}\rightarrow\GL(V)
. According to principles outlined above, there is a tautological system
\tau(\rho,\overline{Y},\beta)
and its Fourier transform
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\overline{Y},\beta)
. We relate in
4.11
the restriction of
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\overline{Y},\beta)
to
Y
with the intrinsically defined module
\mathscr{N}_{Y}^{\beta}
. We then develop therefore a framework, based on the formalism of Lie algebroids and their universal enveloping algebras, to study the vanishing resp. non-vanishing of the module
\mathscr{N}_{Y}^{\beta}
. The first main result in this section is
4.28
which gives a sufficient criterion for
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\overline{Y},\beta)
to be non-zero. Afterwards, this is applied to the more specific case where the variety
Y
is the complement of the zero section of the total space of a line bundle over a variety
X
equipped with an action by a group
G
. Then
L
can, with the choice of a character, be made into a
G^{\prime}
-space, where
G^{\prime}:={\mathds{C}}^{*}\times G
. The second main result is
4.34
, which not only gives sufficient and necessary non-vanishing criteria for the
system
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\overline{Y},\beta)
, but also describes this system, if it is non-zero, as a direct image of one of the modules
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}
introduced earlier in
Section

2
.

In
Section

5
, we derive (via representation theoretic methods) a formula for the complex parameter value
\beta(\mathbf{e})\in{\mathds{C}}

for which the tautological system
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
is non-zero, at least in the case of a semisimple group
G
.
We also give a geometric interpretation of this formula and show that it is compatible with the general criterion of
4.34
.

In
Section

6
, we apply all the previous results in the case where the variety
X
is a homogeneous space, and where the representation
\rho
is in the dual of the space of sections of an equivariant line bundle on
X
. The affine cone of
X
then takes the role of the
G^{\prime}
-invariant space used in the definition of the tautological system
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
. The main result is then
6.14
, showing that if
\beta
is such that
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\neq 0
, then it underlies a pure complex Hodge module for
\beta(\mathbf{e})\notin{\mathds{Z}}
and a rational mixed Hodge module for
\beta(\mathbf{e})\in{\mathds{Z}}_{>0}
. Moreover, we exhibit in
6.15
a functorial description of
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
as a
direct resp. as a proper direct image of a family of (complements of) hyperplane sections of
X
, and in consequence solve the holonomic rank problem as stated in
[
BHL+14
]
and
[
HLZ16
]
in this generalized situation. A major ingredient necessary for the formulation of this functorial description is to determine how the Fourier–Laplace transformation of
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
is related to its restriction to the complement of the origin. The answer to this question depends crucially on whether
\beta(\mathbf{e})
is integral or not; we treat the two cases separately in
Section

6.1
and
Section

6.2
.

Notations:
Throughout, we work over
\mathds{C}
. By
variety
, we mean an integral scheme of finite type over
\mathds{C}
. When we talk about
points
on a variety, we mean closed points unless mentioned otherwise. Our convention for the projective space of a finite-dimensional vector space
V
is
\mathds{P}V:=\Proj\Sym V^{\vee}
, i.e.,
\mathds{P}V
parameterizes one-dimensional
subspaces
of
V
.
For a smooth variety
X
, we let
\mathscr{D}_{X}
be the sheaf of algebraic differential operators on
X
. If not mentioned otherwise, a
\mathscr{D}_{X}
-module is a
quasi-coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module equipped with a left action
by
\mathscr{D}_{X}
. The category of such modules is denoted by
\textup{Mod}_{qc}(\mathscr{D}_{X})
and the corresponding bounded derived category by
D_{qc}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{X})
.
Similarly, let
\textup{Mod}_{h}(\mathscr{D}_{X})
and
D_{h}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{X})
be the category of holonomic
\mathscr{D}_{X}
-modules and its corresponding bounded derived category, respectively.
Throughout, for a morphism
f\colon X\to Y
between smooth varieties over
\mathds{C}
, we denote by
f_{+}\colon D_{qc}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{X})\to D_{qc}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{Y})
and
f^{+}\colon D_{qc}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{Y})\to D_{qc}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{X})
the functors defined by

f_{+}\mathscr{M}:=Rf_{*}(\mathscr{D}_{Y\leftarrow X}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{D}_{X}}\mathscr{M})\quad\text{and}\quad f^{+}\mathscr{N}:=\mathscr{D}_{X\to Y}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{f^{-1}\mathscr{D}_{Y}}f^{-1}\mathscr{N}.

Moreover, we denote by

{\mathbb{D}}\mathscr{M}:=\omega^{\vee}_{X}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}R{\mathcal{H}\!}om_{\mathscr{D}_{X}}(\mathscr{M},\mathscr{D}_{X})[\dim(X)]

the duality functor from
D^{b}_{h}(\mathscr{D}_{X})
to itself; it respects

\textup{Mod}_{h}(\mathscr{D}_{X})
. We then define the functors

f_{\dagger}:={\mathbb{D}}\circ f_{+}\circ{\mathbb{D}}\quad\quad\textup{and}\quad\quad f^{\dagger}:={\mathbb{D}}\circ f^{+}\circ{\mathbb{D}}.

For any variety
X
, let
\textup{MHM}(X)
be the Abelian category of algebraic
(
{\mathds{Q}}
-)mixed Hodge modules on
X
(as defined in
[
Sai88
,
Sai90
]
) and
D^{b}\textup{MHM}(X)
its bounded derived category. For any morphism
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
the functors
f_{+},f_{\dagger}
resp.
f^{\dagger}[\dim(Y)-\dim(X)],f^{+}[\dim(X)-\dim(Y)]
on
D^{b}_{h}(\mathscr{D}_{X})
resp.
D^{b}_{h}(\mathscr{D}_{Y})
lift to functors

f_{*},f_{!}\colon D^{b}\textup{MHM}(X)\rightarrow D^{b}\textup{MHM}(Y)\quad\quad\textup{resp.}\quad\quad f^{*},f^{!}\colon D^{b}\textup{MHM}(Y)\rightarrow D^{b}\textup{MHM}(X).

We also denote by
{\mathbb{D}}
the functor on
D^{b}\textup{MHM}(X)
which lifts the above defined holonomic duality functor on
D^{b}_{h}(\mathscr{D}_{X})
.
Any object
\mathcal{M}\in\textup{MHM}(X)
is a tuple

\mathcal{M}=(\mathscr{M},F_{\bullet},W_{\bullet},K)
where

\mathscr{M}\in\textup{Mod}_{h}(\mathscr{D}_{X})
and
W_{\bullet}\mathscr{M}
is its weight filtration. We denote by

\textup{HM}(X,w)
(or simply
\textup{HM}(X)
if
w
is clear from the context) the full subcategory of objects such that

\gr_{l}(\mathscr{M})=0
for all
l\neq w
; these are the pure Hodge modules of weight
w
.

We will need an extension of the notion of (
{\mathds{Q}}
-)mixed Hodge modules to the category of complex mixed Hodge modules. It can be constructed by first defining
{\mathds{R}}
-mixed Hodge modules,
see
[
Moc15
, Section 13.5]
. Then a filtered
\mathscr{D}
-module
(\mathscr{M},F_{\bullet})
is said to underly a complex mixed Hodge module if it is a direct summand of an
{\mathds{R}}
-mixed Hodge module (
[
DS13
, Definition 3.2.1.]
). We denote the corresponding Abelian category by
\textup{MHM}(X,{\mathds{C}})
, by

D^{b}\textup{MHM}(X,{\mathds{C}})
its bounded derived category and by

\textup{HM}(X,{\mathds{C}},w)
(or
\textup{HM}(X,{\mathds{C}})
for short) the category of pure complex Hodge modules of weight
w
. Many of the known constructions for
{\mathds{R}}
-mixed Hodge modules carry over to the categories
\textup{MHM}(X,{\mathds{C}})
and
\textup{HM}(X,{\mathds{C}})
since they are stable under taking direct summands.
The article
[
DV22
, Section 7.1 and Appendix A]
contains a more detailed discussion of complex Hodge modules.

For any variety
X
, write
a_{X}\colon X\rightarrow\{pt\}
for the map to the point. We denote by

{{}^{H}\!}{\mathds{C}}_{pt}
the trivial complex Hodge structure of dimension
1
. Then

{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{X}:=a_{X}^{*}{{}^{H}\!}{\mathds{C}}_{pt}[\dim(X)],

is a smooth (constant) Hodge module and indeed an object in
\textup{MHM}(X,{\mathds{C}})
. Notice that our notation differs from the convention in
[
Sai88
,
Sai90
]
, where the (
{\mathds{Q}}
-)constant Hodge module of rank
1
is denoted by
{{}^{p}}{\mathds{Q}}_{X}^{H}
.

We will further need a particular smooth (but non-constant) complex Hodge module on the one-dimensional torus
{\mathds{C}}^{*}
. Namely, for any
\beta\in{\mathds{R}}
,
we denote by
\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}
the
\mathscr{D}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}
-module

\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}=\mathscr{D}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}/\mathscr{D}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}(\partial_{t}t+\beta).

We write
{{}^{H}\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}
for the complex Hodge module with underlying
\mathscr{D}
-module equal to
\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}
(placed in cohomological degree zero), and where

\gr^{F}_{p}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}=0
for
p\neq 0
and
\gr^{W}_{i}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}=0
for
i\neq 1
. Its corresponding perverse sheaf
is
{\mathds{V}}[1]
, where
{\mathds{V}}
is the local system of rank
1
on
{\mathds{C}}^{*}
given by the monodromy with eigenvalue
e^{2\pi\sqrt{-1}\beta}
. Again, in the conventions of
[
Sai88
,
Sai90
]
this object would have been denoted by

{{}^{p}}{\mathds{C}}_{X}^{H,\beta}
.

Acknowledgements:
We thank Michel Brion for his assistance finding references for the parabolic version of Borel–Weil and for his explanations about line bundles on homogeneous varieties.
We thank Luis Narváez Macarro for sharing some insights on Lie algebroids with us, especially for his help with the proof of
4.16
.

2
Mixed Hodge modules on line bundles

As a preliminary result, we state and prove for the reader’s convenience the following well known fact about the fundamental group of the complement of the zero section of a line bundle.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
M
be a simply connected complex manifold, i.e.
\pi_{1}(M)=\{e\}
. Let
\pi:L\rightarrow M
be a holomorphic line bundle on
M
, and write

c_{1}(L)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}e_{i}\in H^{2}(M,\mathds{Z})

for some basis
e_{1},\ldots,e_{r}
of
H^{2}(M,\mathds{Z})
.
Denote by
L^{*}
the complement of the zero section of
L
. Then
\pi_{1}(L^{*})=\mathds{Z}/k\mathds{Z}
, where
k=\textup{gcd}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r})
.

Proof.

We first notice that the assumption
\pi_{1}(M)=\{e\}
and the universal coefficient theorem for cohomology implies that
H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})
is free, so the statement of the proposition makes sense. Furthermore,
L^{*}\rightarrow M
is a principal
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-bundle, hence by Milnor’s construction
[
Mil56
]
for the case
G=\mathbb{C}^{*}
there is a classifying space
B:=BG
, a universal principal
G
-bundle
p:E:=EG\rightarrow B
and a map
\varphi:M\rightarrow B
so that
L^{*}
is the pullback of
E
along
\varphi
.

Set
I:=[0,1]
, denote by
\ast
the base point of
B
and by
PB:=\{\gamma\in B^{I}:\gamma(0)=\ast\}
the Moore path space over
B
. Since
B
is path connected
we have the Moore path space fibration for
(B;*)

\Omega B\longrightarrow PB\overset{\rho}{\longrightarrow}B\qquad\rho(\gamma)=\gamma(1).

and analogously for
\pi:PE\rightarrow E
. It can be shown
[
FHT01
, Proposition 2.10]
that there is an action of
G\times_{E}PE
on
PE
, making
\pi:PE\rightarrow B
a
G\times_{E}PE
-fibration. One gets a diagram of fibrations

\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{PE\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{PB\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{B}

with fibers

\textstyle{G}
\textstyle{G\times_{E}PE\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\gamma^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\Omega B}

where
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
are weak homotopy equivalences (see loc. cit.) .

Pulling back this diagram along
\varphi
one gets

\textstyle{L^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\times_{B}PE\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H(\varphi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

where
H(\varphi)
is by definition the homotopy fiber of
\varphi
giving a fibration

H(\varphi)\longrightarrow X\overset{\varphi}{\longrightarrow}B.

Notice that
L^{*}
and
H(\varphi)
are weakly homotopy equivalent, in particular
\pi_{1}(L^{*})\simeq\pi_{1}(H(\varphi))
.

Consider the long exact homotopy sequence of the fibration above

\pi_{2}(X)\longrightarrow\pi_{2}(B)\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(H(\varphi))\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(M)=0

Since
\pi_{1}(M)
and
\pi_{1}(B)
are trivial we have
\pi_{2}(M)\simeq H_{2}(M,\mathds{Z})
and
\pi_{2}(B)\simeq H_{2}(B,\mathds{Z})\simeq\mathds{Z}
. In particular, we get the exact sequence

H_{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})_{\text{free}}\ \longrightarrow H_{2}(B,\mathbb{Z})\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(M)\longrightarrow 0

By duality we get the map
\mathbb{Z}\cdot\theta\simeq H^{2}(B,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})
, where
\theta
is a generator of
H^{2}(B,\mathbb{Z})
. Notice that this map is simply the pullback of cohomology classes along the classifying map
M\rightarrow B
. In order to identify the image of
\theta
, we notice that there is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{B=B\mathbb{C}^{*}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{BU(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The map
BU(1)\rightarrow B
is induced by the inclusion
U(1)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{*}
and is a homotopy equivalence, in particular
H^{*}(B,\mathbb{Z})\simeq H^{*}(BU(1),\mathbb{Z})\simeq\mathbb{Z}[\theta]
with
\deg(\theta)=2
. The map
M\rightarrow BU(1)
is the classifying space of the sphere bundle of
L
. By definiton of the Chern classes the pullback of
\theta
along
M\rightarrow BU(1)
is the first Chern class of
L
. We conclude that, with respect to the dual basis of
e_{1},\ldots,e_{r}
, the map
H_{2}(M,\mathbb{Z})_{\text{free}}\rightarrow H_{2}(B,\mathbb{Z})
is given by
\mathbb{Z}^{r}\overset{(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r})}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{Z}
.

∎

From now on, let
X
be a smooth complex projective variety and let
L\to X
an algebraic line bundle. Unless noted otherwise, we work in the algebraic setting and denote the associated complex manifolds by
X^{\mathrm{an}},L^{\mathrm{an}},\dots
. In particular, when assuming that
\pi_{1}(X^{\mathrm{an}})=\{e\}
, we can apply the above proposition to the case where
M:=X^{\mathrm{an}}
and to the holomorphic line bundle
L^{\mathrm{an}}\rightarrow X^{\mathrm{an}}
. We therefore conclude that
\pi_{1}(L^{*,\mathrm{an}})=\mathds{Z}/k\mathds{Z}
. For the remainder of this paper, we will moreover assume that
L
is a non-trivial line bundle on
X
(in particuler, we assume that
\textup{Pic}(X)\neq 0
). This implies in particular that the number
k
obtain in the previous proposition is different from zero.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
L\rightarrow X
be as above, and let
k
be as in
2.1
.
Choose a rational number
\beta
in
\frac{1}{k}\mathds{Z}
. Consider the representation
\pi_{1}(L^{*,\mathrm{an}})\rightarrow{\mathds{C}}^{*}
given by sending
[1]\in\mathds{Z}/k\mathds{Z}\cong\pi_{1}(L^{*,\mathrm{an}})
to the
k
-th root of unity

e^{-2\pi i\beta}
. This defines a local system on
L^{*,\mathrm{an}}
which we denote by
\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}
. The corresponding
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}
-module with integrable connection
(i.e., the corresponding smooth
\mathscr{D}_{L^{*}}
-module) is denoted
by
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}
. It underlies a complex smooth pure Hodge module on
L^{*}
denoted by
{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}
.

Notice that it follows from this definition that
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}\cong\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta^{\prime}}
for
\beta-\beta^{\prime}\in\mathds{Z}
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Locally, over an open subset
U\subseteq X
trivializing the line bundle,
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}
is isomorphic to the
\mathscr{D}_{\mathds{C}^{*}\times U}
-module

\mathscr{D}_{\mathds{C}^{*}}/(\partial_{t}t+\beta)\boxtimes\mathcal{O}_{U}.

Proof.

Write
L_{U}^{*}
for the restriction of
L^{*}
over the trivializing set
U\subseteq X
. Clearly,
U
is also a trivializing open set for
L^{*}
, i.e., we have
L^{*}_{U}\cong{\mathds{C}}^{*}\times U
. First we note that

\pi_{1}(L_{U}^{*,\mathrm{an}})\cong\pi_{1}({\mathds{C}}^{*,\mathrm{an}}\times U^{\mathrm{an}})=\mathds{Z}\times\pi_{1}(U^{\mathrm{an}}).

Notice that although one can find trivialising sets of the analytic bundle
L^{\mathrm{an}}\rightarrow X^{\mathrm{an}}
which are simply connected, this is not necessarily true for the set
U^{\mathrm{an}}
, which is the analytification of a (Zariski open) trivialising set of the algebraic bundle
L\rightarrow X
.

The group homomorphism
\pi_{1}(L_{U}^{*,\mathrm{an}})\to\pi_{1}(L^{*,\mathrm{an}})
induced by the inclusion is given by

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\pi_{1}(L_{U}^{*,\mathrm{an}})=\mathds{Z}\times\pi_{1}(U^{\mathrm{an}})&\longrightarrow&\mathds{Z}/k\mathds{Z}=\pi_{1}(L^{*,\mathrm{an}})\\
(l,\gamma)&\longmapsto&\overline{l}\end{array}

since
\pi_{1}(X^{\mathrm{an}})=\{e\}
.
Now, the restriction by
L_{U}^{*,\mathrm{an}}\hookrightarrow L^{*,\mathrm{an}}
of
\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}
is given by the representation
\pi_{1}(L_{U}^{*,\mathrm{an}})\rightarrow{\mathds{C}}^{*}
obtained by composing the map
\pi_{1}(L_{U}^{*,\mathrm{an}})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(L^{*,\mathrm{an}})
with the given representation
\pi_{1}(L^{*,\mathrm{an}})\rightarrow{\mathds{C}}^{*}
defining
\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}
. Hence, it sends
(1,\gamma)
to
e^{-2\pi i\beta}
. Therefore, the restriction of
\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}
to
L^{*,\mathrm{an}}_{U}
is isomorphic to
\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{\mathds{C}^{*}}\boxtimes\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{U^{\mathrm{an}}}
, and consequently the restriction of the algebraic
\mathscr{D}_{L^{*}}
-module
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}
to
L_{U}^{*}
is given as stated above.
∎

The following fact about the holonomic dual of
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}
is obvious from the definition, since for smooth objects, the holonomic dual coincides with the dual bundle (with dual connection).

Lemma 2.4
.

We have
{\mathbb{D}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}\cong\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{-\beta}

for all
\beta\in\frac{1}{k}\mathds{Z}
.

The next step is to consider extensions of the
\mathscr{D}_{L^{*}}
-module
\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}
(resp. of the corresponding Hodge module
{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}
) over the zero section of
L\rightarrow X
. For this, we chose a covering

X=\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
by Zariski open subsets over each of which the bundle
L
(as well as its restriction
L^{*}
) trivializes. We write
L_{U_{i}}
and (as above)
L^{*}_{U_{i}}
for the restriction of the bundle
L
and that of
L^{*}
over the open set
U_{i}
. We denote by
j_{i}:L_{U_{i}}\hookrightarrow L
resp. by

\tilde{j}_{i}:L^{*}_{U_{i}}\hookrightarrow L^{*}
be the canonical open embeddings.
By shrinking
U_{i}
if necessary (so that it becomes the complement of a divisor in
X
, and so will be
L_{U_{i}}
in
L
, and
L^{*}_{U_{i}}
in
L^{*}
), it will be convenient to assume that both
j_{i}
and
\tilde{j}_{i}
are affine maps, in particular, the functors
j_{i,\star}
and
\tilde{j}_{i,\star}
are exact for
\star\in\{+,{\dagger}\}
.

Moreover, we write
j_{L}:L^{*}\hookrightarrow L
for the open embedding of the complement of the zero section into
L
. We then have the following cartesian diagram of canonical open embeddings:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{*}_{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{U_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{j}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{j}_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{i}}

(3)

By construction of the module
\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}
, for any
i\in I
, we have an isomorphism
(depending on the choice of a trivialisation of
L
resp. of
L^{*}
over
U_{i}
)

\psi_{i}:\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}\boxtimes\mathcal{O}_{U_{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\tilde{j}_{i}^{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}.

Then we have the following statement

Proposition 2.5
.

1.

The
\mathscr{D}_{L}
-modules
j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}
resp.
j_{L,{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}
underly the mixed Hodge modules
j_{L,*}{{}^{H\!}}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}

resp.
j_{L,!}{{}^{H\!}}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}
on
L
.

2.

If

\beta\notin{\mathds{Z}}
, then

j_{L,*}{{}^{H\!}}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}\cong j_{L,!}{{}^{H\!}}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}\cong j_{L,!*}{{}^{H\!}}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}},

which is pure of weight
\dim(X)+1
.

3.

For any
\beta\in\frac{1}{k}{\mathds{Z}}
the following isomorphisms hold in
\textup{MHM}(L)

j_{L,*}{{}^{H\!}}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}\cong j_{i,!*}j_{i}^{*}j_{L,*}{{}^{H\!}}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}},\quad\quad\textup{and}\quad\quad j_{L,!}{{}^{H\!}}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}\cong j_{i,!*}j_{i}^{*}j_{L,!}{{}^{H\!}}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}},

i.e., these mixed Hodge modules are the minimal extensions of their restrictions to open sets
L_{U_{i}}\subset L
.

Proof.

1.

This is obvious.

2.

Notice that we have a well defined morphism

j_{L,{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}\longrightarrow j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}.

It suffices to show that if
\beta\notin\mathds{Z}
, then this is an isomorphism in
\textup{Mod}(\mathscr{D}_{L})
. This is a local statement, therefore, we can reduce the proof to show that for any
i\in I
, the morphism

j_{i}^{+}j_{L,{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}\longrightarrow j_{i}^{+}j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}.

is an isomorphism. Since
j_{i}^{+}\cong j_{i}^{\dagger}
, this is equivalent by base change (see diagram (
3
)) to show that

\tilde{j}_{L,{\dagger}}\tilde{j}_{i}^{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}\longrightarrow\tilde{j}_{L,+}\tilde{j}_{i}^{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}

is an isomorphism. Since
\tilde{j}_{i}^{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}
is isomorphic to

\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}\boxtimes\mathcal{O}_{U_{i}}
via
\psi_{i}
, and since the functors
\tilde{j}_{L,+}
resp.
\tilde{j}_{L,{\dagger}}
correspond to
(j_{\mathds{C}}\times\id_{U_{i}})_{+}
resp.
(j_{\mathds{C}}\times\id_{U_{i}})_{\dagger}
under this isomorphism, the statement reduces to the well-known fact that

j_{{\mathds{C}},+}\mathcal{O}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}^{\beta}\cong j_{{\mathds{C}},{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}^{\beta}\cong j_{{\mathds{C}},{\dagger}+}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}
for
\beta\notin{\mathds{Z}}
.

3.

Again it suffice to show the statement on the level of
\mathscr{D}_{L}
-module, i.e., we need to show that for all
i\in I
we have

j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}\cong j_{i,{\dagger}+}j_{i}^{+}j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}\quad\quad\textup{and}\quad\quad j_{L,{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}\cong j_{i,{\dagger}+}j_{i}^{+}j_{L,{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}

(4)

Let us prove the first statement concerning the extension
j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}
, the proof of the second one is similar. Fix
i\in I
. Then for any
r\in I\backslash\{i\}
, we obtain an isomorphism

j_{r}^{+}j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}\cong j_{r}^{+}j_{i,{\dagger}+}j_{i}^{+}j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}

(5)

by an argument similar to point 2. above. Namely, in order to show (
5
), it suffices by base change (notice that all functors involved are exact, so the base change property also holds for the intermediate extension) to prove

j_{r,L,+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}_{U_{r}}}^{\beta}\cong j_{ir,{\dagger}+}j_{ir}^{+}j_{r,L,+}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L_{U_{r}}^{*}}

where now
j_{r,L}:L^{*}_{U_{r}}\hookrightarrow L_{U_{r}}
and where

j_{ir}:L_{U_{i}\cap U_{r}}\hookrightarrow L_{U_{r}}
. However, since both
L^{*}
and
L
trivializes over
U_{r}
and since the module
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}
resp. the extension
j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}
is isomorphic to the exterior product
\mathcal{O}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}^{\beta}\boxtimes\mathcal{O}_{U_{r}}
resp. to
j_{{\mathds{C}},+}\mathcal{O}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}^{\beta}\boxtimes\mathcal{O}_{U_{r}}
(and since obviously

\mathcal{O}_{U_{r}}
is the minimal extension of
\mathcal{O}_{U_{i}\cap U_{r}}
), we obtain the existence of the isomorphism (
5
). Now it is a tedious but straightforward check that these isomorphisms are compatible on intersections of trivializing open sets, hence they yield the desired isomorphism

j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}\cong j_{i,{\dagger}+}j_{i}^{+}j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}.

∎

3
Fourier–Laplace transformation

The purpose of this section is twofold: First we recall a few basic properties of general Fourier-Laplace transformations on (not necessarily trivial) vector bundles. We then apply these constructions to study a (complex of)
\mathscr{D}
-module(s) that generically computes cohomology groups of hyperplane sections of projective varieties. These results are used later in
Section

6
for the special case of
homogeneous spaces and their corresponding tautological systems.

3.1
Fourier–Laplace transformation on vector bundles

Definition 3.1
.

Given a vector bundle
E\to X
on a smooth variety
X
, we consider the canonical projections
p_{1}\colon E\times_{X}E^{\vee}\to E
and
p_{2}\colon E\times_{X}E^{\vee}\to E^{\vee}
. Let
\alpha\colon E\times_{X}E^{\vee}\to\mathds{C}\times X\to\mathds{C}
be the natural pairing and denote
\mathscr{K}:=\alpha^{+}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathds{C}}/\mathscr{D}_{\mathds{C}}(\partial_{t}+1))
.
The
Fourier–Laplace transformation
is the functor
\FL_{X}^{E}\colon D_{qc}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{E})\to D_{qc}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{E^{\vee}})
given as

\FL_{X}^{E}(\mathscr{M}):=p_{2,+}(p_{1}^{+}\mathscr{M}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{E\!\times\!E^{\vee}}}\mathscr{K}).

We first note a well-known fact concerning the behavior of the Fourier-Laplace transformation with respect to the holonomic duality functor.

Lemma 3.2
.

We have

c^{+}\circ\FL_{X}^{E}\circ{\mathbb{D}}\cong{\mathbb{D}}\circ\FL_{X}^{E}

as functors from
D^{b}_{c}(\mathscr{D}_{E})\to D^{b}_{c}(\mathscr{D}_{E^{\vee}})
,
where
c:E^{\vee}\to E^{\vee}
is the
automorphism given by fiberwise negation.

Proof.

This can be shown exactly as in
[
Dai00
, Corollaire 2.2.2.1., 4)]
(see especially loc.cit, Proposition 2.2.3.2)
∎

We proceed with the following two basic properties that follow rather directly from the projection formula and base change.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\varphi\colon E\to F
be a morphism of vector bundles over
X
and denote by
\varphi^{\vee}\colon F^{\vee}\to E^{\vee}
the induced morphism of dual vector bundles. Then for
\star\in\{+,{\dagger}\}
we have

\FL_{X}^{F}\circ\varphi_{\star}\cong\varphi^{\vee,\star}\circ\FL_{X}^{E}

as functors
D_{c}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{E})\to D_{c}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{F^{\vee}})
.

Proof.

We only show the case
\star=+
from which the case
\star={\dagger}
follows directly using
3.2
.
We denote
\mathscr{K}^{E}:=(\alpha^{E})^{+}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathds{C}}/\mathscr{D}_{\mathds{C}}(\partial_{t}+1))
and
\mathscr{K}^{F}:=(\alpha^{F})^{+}(\mathscr{D}_{\mathds{C}}/\mathscr{D}_{\mathds{C}}(\partial_{t}+1))
, where
\alpha^{E}\colon E\times_{X}E^{\vee}\to\mathds{C}
and
\alpha^{F}\colon F\times_{X}F^{\vee}\to\mathds{C}
are the natural pairings. Moreover, denote by
q_{1}\colon E\times_{X}F^{\vee}\to E
and
q_{2}\colon E\times_{X}F^{\vee}\to F^{\vee}
the projections onto the first and second factor. Consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\times_{X}E^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\times_{X}F^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\times_{X}F^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phantom{F\times_{X}F^{\vee}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{E}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{E}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{E}\times\varphi^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi\times\id_{F^{\vee}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\vee}}

whose squares are cartesian. For every
\mathscr{M}\in D^{b}_{c}(\mathscr{D}_{E})
, we have:

\displaystyle\FL_{X}^{F}(\varphi_{+}\mathscr{M})

\displaystyle=p_{2,+}^{F}(p_{1}^{F,+}\varphi_{+}\mathscr{M}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{F\!\times\!F^{\vee}}}\mathscr{K}^{F})

\displaystyle\cong p_{2,+}^{F}\big((\varphi\times\id_{F^{\vee}})_{+}q_{1}^{+}\mathscr{M}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{F\!\times\!F^{\vee}}}\mathscr{K}^{F}\big)

(base change)

\displaystyle\cong p_{2,+}^{F}(\varphi\times\id_{F^{\vee}})_{+}\big(q_{1}^{+}\mathscr{M}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{E\!\times\!F^{\vee}}}(\varphi\times\id_{F^{\vee}})^{+}\mathscr{K}^{F}\big)

(projection formula)

\displaystyle\cong q_{2,+}\big(q_{1}^{+}\mathscr{M}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{E\!\times\!F^{\vee}}}(\varphi\times\id_{F^{\vee}})^{+}\mathscr{K}^{F}\big)

(
q_{2}=p_{2}^{F}\circ(\varphi\times\id_{F^{\vee}})
)

\displaystyle\cong q_{2,+}\big(q_{1}^{+}\mathscr{M}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{E\!\times\!F^{\vee}}}(\id_{E}\times\varphi^{\vee})^{+}\mathscr{K}^{E}\big)

(
\alpha^{E}\circ(\id_{E}\times\varphi^{\vee})=\alpha^{F}\circ(\varphi\times\id_{F^{\vee}})
)

\displaystyle\cong q_{2,+}\big((\id_{E}\times\varphi^{\vee})^{+}p_{1}^{E,+}\mathscr{M}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{E\!\times\!F^{\vee}}}(\id_{E}\times\varphi^{\vee})^{+}\mathscr{K}^{E}\big)

(
q_{1}=p_{1}^{E}\circ(\id_{E}\times\varphi^{\vee})
)

\displaystyle\cong\varphi^{\vee}_{+}p_{2}^{E,+}(p_{1}^{E,+}\mathscr{M}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{E\!\times\!E^{\vee}}}\mathscr{K}^{E})

(base change)

\displaystyle=\varphi^{\vee}_{+}\FL_{X}^{E}(\mathscr{M}).

Lemma 3.4
.

Consider a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}

where the vertical arrows are vector bundles over smooth varieties. Denote by
g^{\vee}\colon E^{\vee}\to F^{\vee}
the corresponding morphism of dual vector bundles. Then for

\star=\{+,{\dagger}\}
we have

\FL_{Y}^{F}\circ g_{\star}\cong g^{\vee}_{\star}\circ\FL_{X}^{E}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\FL_{X}^{E}\circ g^{\star}\cong g^{\vee,\star}\circ\FL_{Y}^{F}

as functors
D_{c}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{E})\to D_{c}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{F^{\vee}})
and
D_{c}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{F})\to D_{c}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{E^{\vee}})
, respectively.

Proof.

Again we restrict to the case

\star=+
, and invoke duality to deduce the corresponding statements for
\star={\dagger}
.
We use notations as in the proof of
3.3
. Note that we have the following commutative diagram with cartesian squares:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\times_{X}E^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\times_{Y}F^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\vee}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}^{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}^{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\times g^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{1}^{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{2}^{F}}

For every
\mathscr{M}\in D_{qc}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{E})
, we have:

\displaystyle\FL_{Y}^{F}(g_{+}\mathscr{M})

\displaystyle=p_{2,+}^{F}(p_{1}^{F,+}g_{+}\mathscr{M}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{F\!\times_{\!Y}\!F^{\vee}}}\mathscr{K}^{F})

\displaystyle\cong p_{2,+}^{F}\big((g\times g^{\vee})_{+}p_{1}^{E,+}\mathscr{M}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{F\!\times_{\!Y}\!F^{\vee}}}\mathscr{K}^{F}\big)

(base change)

\displaystyle\cong p_{2,+}^{F}(g\times g^{\vee})_{+}\big(p_{1}^{E,+}\mathscr{M}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{E\!\times_{\!X}\!E^{\vee}}}(g\times g^{\vee})^{+}\mathscr{K}^{F}\big)

(projection formula)

\displaystyle\cong g^{\vee}_{+}p_{2,+}^{E}\big(p_{1}^{E,+}\mathscr{M}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{E\!\times_{\!X}\!E^{\vee}}}(g\times g^{\vee})^{+}\mathscr{K}^{F}\big)

(
p_{2}^{F}\circ(g\times g^{\vee})=g^{\vee}\circ p_{2}^{E}
)

\displaystyle\cong g^{\vee}_{+}p_{2,+}^{E}(p_{1}^{E,+}\mathscr{M}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{E\!\times_{\!X}\!E^{\vee}}}\mathscr{K}^{E})

(
\alpha^{E}=\alpha^{F}\circ(g\times g^{\vee})
)

\displaystyle=g_{+}^{\vee}\FL_{X}^{E}(\mathscr{M}).

Similarly, for
\mathscr{N}\in D_{qc}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{F})
, we get:

\displaystyle\FL_{X}^{E}(g^{+}\mathscr{N})

\displaystyle=p_{2,+}^{E}(p_{1}^{E,+}g^{+}\mathscr{N}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{E\!\times_{\!X}\!E^{\vee}}}\mathscr{K}^{E})

\displaystyle\cong p_{2,+}^{E}\big((g\times g^{\vee})^{+}p_{1}^{F,+}\mathscr{N}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{E\!\times_{\!X}\!E^{\vee}}}\mathscr{K}^{E}\big)

(
g\circ p_{1}^{E}=p_{1}^{F}\circ(g\times g^{\vee})
)

\displaystyle\cong p_{2,+}^{E}\big((g\times g^{\vee})^{+}p_{1}^{F,+}\mathscr{N}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{E\!\times_{\!X}\!E^{\vee}}}(g\times g^{\vee})^{+}\mathscr{K}^{F}\big)

(
\alpha^{E}=\alpha^{F}\circ(g\times g^{\vee})
)

\displaystyle\cong g^{\vee,+}p_{2,+}^{F}\big(p_{1}^{F,+}\mathscr{N}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathcal{O}_{F\!\times_{\!Y}\!F^{\vee}}}\mathscr{K}^{F}\big)

(base change)

\displaystyle=g^{\vee,+}\FL_{Y}^{F}(\mathscr{N}).

In the following, we wish to relate Fourier–Laplace transforms on
vector bundles with classical Fourier–Laplace transforms on a finite-dimensional vector space (which is the special case of a vector bundle over a point). For this, we consider the following situation: Let
\pi\colon E\to X
be a vector bundle on a smooth variety and denote by
\mathcal{E}
its sheaf of sections, i.e.,
E=\Tot(\mathcal{E}):=\Spec_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\Sym^{\bullet}\mathcal{E}^{\vee}
. Let
W\subseteq\Gamma(X,\mathcal{E})
be a non-zero finite-dimensional vector space of global sections of
E
and let
V:=W^{\vee}
be its dual vector space. There are natural bundle morphisms
ev\colon W\times X\to E
and
ev^{\vee}\colon E^{\vee}\to V\times X
, where
E^{\vee}
denotes the dual vector bundle to
E
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
W
be a finite-dimensional space of global sections of a vector bundle
E\to X
on a smooth variety. Let
V
denote its dual vector space. If
a_{V}\colon V\times X\to V
and
a_{W}\colon W\times X\to W
denote the projections onto the first factors, we have

\FL^{V}(a_{V,+}ev^{\vee}_{+}\mathscr{M})\cong a_{W,+}ev^{+}\FL_{X}^{E^{\vee}}(\mathscr{M})

for all
\mathscr{M}\in D_{qc}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{E^{\vee}})
.

Proof.

The claim follows from
3.3
and
3.4
considering the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Spec\mathds{C}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}

∎

3.2
Fourier–Laplace transform of extensions of
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}

We now determine the Fourier–Laplace transform of the
\mathscr{D}_{L^{*}}
-modules
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}
defined in
Section

2
, where
L^{*}
is the complement of the zero section of a line bundle
\pi\colon L\to X
. Recall that the definition of
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}
depends on the choice of a line bundle
F
with
L=F^{\otimes k}
for some
k\in\mathds{Z}
satisfying
k\beta\in\mathds{Z}
.

Note that the dual line bundle
\pi^{\vee}\colon L^{\vee}\to X
is the
(-k)
-th tensor power
F^{\otimes(-k)}
. In what follows, we will consider the
\mathscr{D}_{L^{\vee,*}}
-module
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{-\beta}
whose definition we always base on the choice of
F
as a
(-k)
-th root of
L^{\vee}
(or, equivalently, based on
F^{\vee}
as a
k
-th root of
L^{\vee}
).

We denote by
j_{L}\colon L^{*}\hookrightarrow L
and
j_{L^{\vee}}\colon L^{\vee,*}\hookrightarrow L^{\vee}
the open embeddings from the complements of the zero section into
L
and
L^{\vee}
, respectively.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\beta\in\mathds{C}
with
k\beta\in\mathds{Z}
. Then

\FL_{X}^{L}(j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta})\cong j_{L^{\vee},{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{-\beta}.

Proof.

We know (see (
4
)) that

j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}\cong j_{i,{\dagger}+}j_{i}^{+}j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}\quad\quad\textup{and}\quad\quad j_{L,{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}\cong j_{i,{\dagger}+}j_{i}^{+}j_{L,{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}

(Recall diagram (
3
) for the maps involved in these isomorphisms).
Moreover, we have trivially

\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}\cong\tilde{j}_{i,{\dagger}+}\tilde{j}_{i}^{+}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}},

since
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}
is a smooth
\mathscr{D}_{L^{*}}
-module. By base change, this implies that

j_{L,+}\tilde{j}_{i,{\dagger}+}\tilde{j}_{i}^{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}\cong j_{i,{\dagger}+}\tilde{j}_{+}\tilde{j}_{i}^{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}

and

j_{L,{\dagger}}\tilde{j}_{i,{\dagger}+}\tilde{j}_{i}^{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}\cong j_{i,{\dagger}+}\tilde{j}_{\dagger}\tilde{j}_{i}^{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}.

Similar statements hold for
\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{-\beta}
and its extensions to
\mathscr{D}_{L^{\vee,*}}
-modules (they involve the canonical open embeddings
j_{i}^{\vee}:L^{\vee}_{U_{i}}\hookrightarrow L^{\vee}
on the dual bundle).

By
3.4
, we have
\FL^{L}_{X}\circ j_{i,\ast}=j^{\vee}_{i,\ast}\circ\FL^{L_{U_{i}}}_{U_{i}}
for
\ast\in\{+,{\dagger},\}
. Moreover, since
all of the four functors
\FL^{L}_{X},j_{i,\ast},j^{\vee}_{i,\ast},\FL^{L_{U_{i}}}_{U_{i}}

are exact, we also obtain
\FL^{L}_{X}\circ j_{i,{\dagger}+}=j^{\vee}_{i,{\dagger}+}\circ\FL^{L_{U_{i}}}_{U_{i}}
.
It follows that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\FL^{L}_{X}(j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta})&\cong&\displaystyle j^{\vee}_{i,{\dagger}+}\FL^{L}_{X}j_{i}^{+}j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}\\
\\
&\cong&\displaystyle j^{\vee}_{i,{\dagger}+}\FL^{L_{U_{i}}}_{U_{i}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j_{{\mathds{C}},+}\mathcal{O}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}^{\beta}\boxtimes\mathcal{O}_{U_{i}}}}\right)\\
\\
&\cong&\displaystyle j^{\vee}_{i,{\dagger}+}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\FL^{{\mathds{C}}}(j_{{\mathds{C}},+}\mathcal{O}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}^{\beta})\boxtimes\mathcal{O}_{U_{i}}}}\right)\\
\\
&\cong&\displaystyle j^{\vee}_{i,{\dagger}+}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j_{{\mathds{C}},{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}_{{\mathds{C}}^{*}}^{-\beta}\boxtimes\mathcal{O}_{U_{i}}}}\right)\\
\\
&\cong&\displaystyle j^{\vee}_{i,{\dagger}+}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j^{\vee,+}_{i}j_{L^{\vee},{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}^{-\beta}_{L^{\vee,*}}}}\right)\\
\\
&\cong&\displaystyle j_{L^{\vee},{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}^{-\beta}_{L^{\vee,*}}.\end{array}

∎

Corollary 3.7
.

Let
k\in\mathds{Z}
and let
\beta\in\mathds{R}
with
k\beta\in\mathds{Z}
. Then the Fourier–Laplace transform on
L
of the
\mathscr{D}_{L}
-module
j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}
can be equipped with the structure of a complex mixed Hodge module which is pure of weight
\dim(X)+1
if
\beta\notin{\mathds{Z}}
.

Proof.

We have just seen in the previous
3.6
that

\FL_{X}^{L}(j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta})\cong j_{L^{\vee},{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{-\beta}.

On the other hand, we know by
2.5
that
j_{L^{\vee},{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{-\beta}
underlies the the object

j_{L^{\vee},!}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{L^{\vee,*}}\in\textup{MHM}(L^{\vee},{\mathds{C}}),

and that it is pure if
\beta\notin{\mathds{Z}}
.
∎

3.3
Twisted cohomology of hyperplane sections

In this subsection, we describe a complex of
\mathscr{D}
-modules that generically computes certain twisted cohomologies of hyperplane sections of our variety
X
(resp. the complement of those). We show that it underlies an object in the derived category of mixed Hodge modules. In the more specific situation studied later in
Section

6
, when
X
arises a homogeneous space, these
\mathscr{D}
-modules will appear as tautological systems.

With the notations from before, we fix a non-zero finite-dimensional subspace
W
of
\Gamma(X,\mathscr{L})
.
Let
V:=W^{\vee}
denote its dual vector space. The linear system
W
on
X
defines a rational map
g\colon X\dashrightarrow\mathds{P}V
. The natural evaluation morphism

ev\colon W\times X\to L,

(6)

is a morphism of vector bundles over
X
and it induces a dual bundle morphism

ev^{\vee}\colon L^{\vee}\to V\times X.

The following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{P}V\times X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\times\id_{X}}

If the linear system
W
is base-point-free, then
g\colon X\to\mathds{P}V
is a morphism and
ev^{\vee}
restricts to a morphism

\widetilde{ev}^{\vee}\colon L^{\vee,*}\to(V\setminus\{0\})\times X

of complements of zero sections. In this case, we have the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\vee,*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V\setminus\{0\})\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\setminus\{0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{P}V\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{P}V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{ev}^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{L^{\vee}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\times\id_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\times\id_{X}}

If, moreover, the linear system
W
separates points and tangent directions (in particular,
\mathscr{L}
is very ample in this case), then
g\colon X\to\mathds{P}V
is a locally closed embedding. In this case,
L^{\vee,*}
is isomorphic to
\hat{X}\setminus\{0\}
, where
\hat{X}\subseteq V
is the affine cone over
g(X)\subseteq\mathds{P}V
, and
L^{\vee}
is the blow-up of
\hat{X}
in the origin:
L^{\vee}\cong\operatorname{Bl}_{\{0\}}\hat{X}
.
We denote further by
\mathcal{Y}:=ev^{-1}(0)
the inverse image of the zero section of
L
, by
\mathcal{U}:=(W\times X)\backslash\mathcal{Y}
its complement, and we write
a_{\mathcal{Y}}:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow W
resp.
a_{\mathcal{U}}:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow W
for the restrictions of the
projection
a_{W}\colon W\times X\to W
to
\mathcal{Y}
resp. to
\mathcal{U}
.

Proposition 3.8
.

Assume
L
to be very ample and let
W\subseteq H^{0}(X,\mathscr{L})
be a finite-dimensional linear system defining a locally closed embedding
g\colon X\hookrightarrow\mathds{P}V
, where
V:=W^{\vee}
. Let
\hat{\iota}\colon L^{\vee,*}\cong\hat{X}\setminus\{0\}\hookrightarrow V
denote the locally closed embedding of the punctured affine cone over
X
into
V
. Then we have the following.

1.

For all
\beta\in\mathds{C}
with
k\beta\in\mathds{Z}
, the complexes of
\mathscr{D}_{W}
-modules

\FL^{V}(\hat{\iota}_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{\beta})\quad\quad\textup{and}\quad\quad\FL^{V}(\hat{\iota}_{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{-\beta})

underly elements of
D^{b}\textup{MHM}(W,{\mathds{C}})

that we denote by
{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}
and by
{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{-\beta}
, respectively. We have

{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}\cong{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta+\ell}\quad\quad\textup{and}\quad\quad{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{-\beta}\cong{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{-\beta+\ell}

for any
\ell\in{\mathds{Z}}
.

2.

For
\beta\in{\mathds{Z}}
, the complexes
\FL^{V}(\hat{\iota}_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{\beta})
and
\FL^{V}(\hat{\iota}_{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{-\beta})
underly elements in
\textup{MHM}(W)

that we denote unambiguously by
{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}
resp. by
{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}
.

3.

For
\beta\notin{\mathds{Z}}
, we have an isomorphism

{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}\cong{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}
. If
X
is projective, then the cohomology modules

H^{i}({{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta})
are pure Hodge modules of weight
\dim(X)+\dim(W)+i
.

4.

Let
\beta\in{\mathds{Z}}
and assume again that
X
is projective. Then for any
k\in{\mathds{Z}}
,
there exists morphisms in the abelian category of mixed Hodge modules

H^{k}(a_{\mathcal{Y},*}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{\mathcal{Y}})\longrightarrow H^{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}}}\right)\quad\text{resp.}\quad H^{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}}}\right)\longrightarrow H^{k}(a_{\mathcal{Y},*}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{\mathcal{Y}})(-1)

with constant kernel of weight
k+\dim X+\dim W-1
resp.
k+\dim X+\dim W
and constant cokernel of weight
k+\dim X+\dim W
resp.
k+\dim X+\dim W+1
.
In particular there are the following weight estimates for
{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}
and
{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}
:

\displaystyle\gr^{W}_{\ell}(H^{k}({{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}))=0

\displaystyle\quad\text{for}\quad\ell\neq k+\dim W+\dim X-1,k+\dim W+\dim X,

\displaystyle\gr^{W}_{\ell}(H^{k}({{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}))=0

\displaystyle\quad\text{for}\quad\ell\neq k+\dim W+\dim X,k+\dim W+\dim X+1.

Proof.

1.

We start by showing the statement for
{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}
. For this purpose, we combine
3.5
and
3.6
to get a purely functorial description of this complex of
\mathscr{D}_{W}
-modules not involving Fourier–Laplace transforms, namely

\begin{array}[]{rcll}&&\FL^{V}(\hat{\iota}_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{\beta})\\
&\cong&\FL^{V}(a_{V,+}ev^{\vee}_{+}j_{{L^{\vee}},+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{\beta})\\
&\cong&a_{W,+}ev^{+}\FL_{X}^{L^{\vee}}(j_{{L^{\vee}},+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{\beta})&{\small\textup{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:FLTransfer}}}\\
&\cong&a_{W,+}ev^{+}j_{L,{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{-\beta}&{\small\textup{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:FLofObeta}}}\\
&\cong&a_{W,+}ev^{\dagger}j_{L,{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{-\beta}&{\small ev\textup{ is smooth}}\\
&=&a_{W,+}\,ev^{\dagger}[\dim L-\dim(W\times X)]\,j_{L,{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{-\beta}[\dim W-1],\end{array}

(7)

where the last equality is due to the obvious dimension count
\dim(L)=\dim(X)+1
.

Since
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{-\beta}
underlies the complex pure Hodge module
{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{L^{*}}
(see
2.2
), we obtain that

{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}:=a_{W,*}ev^{*}j_{L,!}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{L^{*}}[\dim W-1]\in D^{b}\textup{MHM}(W,{\mathds{C}}).

(8)

Define

{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{-\beta}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathbb{D}}\,{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}}}\right)(\dim(W\times X))

where
{\mathbb{D}}
is the duality functor in
\textup{MHM}(W,{\mathds{C}})
as recalled in the introduction. Clearly, the complex of
\mathscr{D}_{W}
-modules that underlies
{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{-\beta}
is then
{\mathbb{D}}\FL^{V}(\hat{\iota}_{+}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{\vee,*}})
, where this time
{\mathbb{D}}
is the holonomic duality functor on
\mathscr{D}_{W}
-modules.

On the spaces
V
and
L^{\vee,*}
, we consider the isomorphisms
c_{V}
and
c_{L^{\vee,*}}
given by multiplication by
-1
(in all variables for
c_{V}
and fibrewise for
c_{L^{\vee,*}}
). Then since the Fourier transformation
\FL^{V}
and the holonomic duality commute up to the action of
c_{V}
(i.e., since

{\mathbb{D}}\,\circ\FL^{V}\cong\FL^{V}\circ{\mathbb{D}}\circ c_{V}^{+}
), we obtain the following isomorphisms in
D^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{W})
for the complex of
\mathscr{D}_{W}
-modules underlying
{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{-\beta}
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{D}}\FL^{V}(\hat{\iota}_{+}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{\vee,*}})

\displaystyle\simeq\FL^{V}{\mathbb{D}}\,c^{+}_{V}(\hat{\iota}_{+}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{\vee,*}})

\displaystyle\simeq\FL^{V}{\mathbb{D}}(\hat{\iota}_{+}c^{+}_{L^{\vee,*}}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{\vee,*}})

\displaystyle{\small\textup{since }c_{V}\circ\hat{\iota}=\hat{\iota}\circ c_{L^{\vee,*}}\textup{ by definition of }\hat{\iota}}

\displaystyle\simeq\FL^{V}{\mathbb{D}}(\hat{\iota}_{+}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{\vee,*}})

\displaystyle{\small\exists\textup{ isomorphism }c^{+}_{L^{\vee,*}}\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{\vee,*}}\cong\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{\vee,*}}}

\displaystyle\simeq\FL^{V}(\hat{\iota}_{\dagger}{\mathbb{D}}\,\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{\vee,*}})

\displaystyle{\small{\mathbb{D}}\,\hat{\iota}_{+}\cong\hat{\iota}_{\dagger}\,{\mathbb{D}}}

\displaystyle\simeq\FL^{V}(\hat{\iota}_{\dagger}\mathcal{O}^{-\beta}_{L^{\vee,*}})

\displaystyle{\small{\mathbb{D}}\,\mathcal{O}^{\beta}_{L^{\vee,*}}\cong\mathcal{O}^{-\beta}_{L^{\vee,*}}\textup{ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:OLbetaDual}}}.

This shows that the underlying complex of
\mathscr{D}_{W}
-modules of
{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{-\beta}
is
\FL^{V}(\hat{\iota}_{\dagger}\mathcal{O}^{-\beta}_{L^{\vee,*}})
, as claimed.

The second statement follows directly from the fact that
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}\cong\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta^{\prime}}
for
\beta-\beta^{\prime}\in\mathds{Z}
.

2.

For
\beta\in{\mathds{Z}}
, we have
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{-\beta}=\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}
, which underlies an element in
\textup{MHM}(L^{*})
, and by the above argument we get that

{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L},{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}\in D^{b}\textup{MHM}(W)
.

3.

Recall from (
7
) above that

\FL^{V}(\hat{\iota}_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{-\beta})\cong a_{W,+}ev^{\dagger}j_{L,{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta}.

Applying the holonomic duality functor yields

{\mathbb{D}}\FL^{V}(\hat{\iota}_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{-\beta})\cong a_{W,+}ev^{\dagger}j_{L,+}{\mathbb{D}}\,\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta},\cong a_{W,+}ev^{\dagger}j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{-\beta},

since
a_{W,{\dagger}}\cong a_{W,+}
(
a_{W}
is proper) and since
ev^{+}\cong ev^{\dagger}
(
ev
is smooth). Now if
\beta\notin{\mathds{Z}}
, by using
2.5
, we have
j_{L,+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{-\beta}\cong j_{L,{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{-\beta}
, and thus we obtain

{\mathbb{D}}\FL^{V}(\hat{\iota}_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{-\beta})\cong a_{W,+}ev^{\dagger}j_{L,{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{-\beta}\cong\FL^{V}(\hat{\iota}_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{\beta}),

from which we deduce an isomorphism

{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}\cong{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}

in
D^{b}\textup{MHM}(W,{\mathds{C}})
.

Moreover, under the assumption that
\beta\notin{\mathds{Z}}
, we have seen in
3.7
that
j_{L,!}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{L^{*}}
is pure (of weight
\dim(X)+1
). Since the morphism
ev
is smooth, and since
a_{W}
is projective here, the second assertion thus follows from
[
Sai88
, Théorème 1]
.

4.

Recall that we denoted by
j_{L}\colon L^{*}\rightarrow L
the inclusion of the complements of the zero section and denote by
i_{L}\colon X\rightarrow L
the inclusion of the zero section of
L
. There is the following adjunction triangle

j_{L,!}\,j_{L}^{-1}\,{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{L}\longrightarrow\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{L}\longrightarrow i_{L,!}i^{-1}_{L}\,{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{L}\overset{+1}{\longrightarrow}

Since
i^{-1}_{L}\,{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{L}=^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{X}[1]
we get the triangle

i_{L,!}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{X}\longrightarrow j_{L,!}\,j_{L}^{-1}\,{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{L}\longrightarrow\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{L}\overset{+1}{\longrightarrow}

(9)

Since the map
j_{L}
is affine, the functor
j_{L,!}
from
\textup{MHM}(L^{*})
to
\textup{MHM}(L)
is exact and
H^{0}(j_{L_{!}}{{}^{H\!}}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{L^{*}})
is the only non-zero cohomology. Therefore we get the short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow i_{L,!}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{X}\longrightarrow H^{0}(j_{L,!}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{L^{*}})\longrightarrow\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{L}\longrightarrow 0

We have the following diagram with cartesian squares

\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\mathcal{Y}}}
\textstyle{X\times W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{ev}
\textstyle{\mathcal{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{L}
\textstyle{L^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Applying the exact functor
ev^{*}[\dim W-1]
to the short exact sequence (
9
) we get the short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow i_{\mathcal{Y},!}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{\mathcal{Y}}\longrightarrow H^{0}(ev^{*}j_{L,!}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{L^{*}}[\dim W-1])\longrightarrow\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{X\times W}\longrightarrow 0

(10)

Notice that
i_{\mathcal{Y},!}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{\mathcal{Y}}
is pure of weight
\dim X+\dim W-1
and that
{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{X\times W}
is pure of weight
\dim X+\dim W
. We apply the functor
a_{W,*}
to (
10
) and get

H^{k-1}(a_{W,*}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{X\times W})\rightarrow H^{k}(a_{\mathcal{Y},*}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{\mathcal{Y}})\rightarrow H^{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}}}\right)\rightarrow H^{k}(a_{W,*}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{X\times W}).

(11)

Since
H^{k}(a_{\mathcal{Y},*}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{\mathcal{Y}})
is pure of weight
k+\dim X+\dim W-1
and the constant mixed Hodge module
H^{k}(a_{W,*}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{X\times W})
is pure of weight
k+\dim X+\dim W
we conclude that

\gr^{W}_{\ell}(H^{k}({{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}))=0\quad\text{for}\quad\ell\neq k+\dim W+\dim X-1,k+\dim W+\dim X.

and there exists a map
H^{k}(a_{\mathcal{Y},*}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{\mathcal{Y}})\rightarrow H^{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}}}\right)
with constant kernel and cokernel.
Applying
\mathbb{D}
to the sequence (
11
) and doing a Tate-twist by
-(\dim X\times W)
we get for
m=-k

H^{m}(a_{W,*}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{X\times W})\rightarrow H^{m}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}}}\right)\rightarrow H^{m}(a_{\mathcal{Y},*}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{\mathcal{Y}})(-1)\rightarrow H^{m+1}(a_{W,*}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{X\times W})

Since
H^{k}(a_{\mathcal{Y},*}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{\mathcal{Y}})
is pure of weight
k+\dim X+\dim W+1
we conclude that

\gr^{W}_{\ell}(H^{m}({{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}))=0\quad\text{for}\quad\ell\neq m+\dim W+\dim X,m+\dim W+\dim X+1.

and there exists a map
H^{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}}}\right)\rightarrow H^{k}(a_{\mathcal{Y},*}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{\mathcal{Y}})(-1)
with constant kernel and cokernel.

∎

We will discuss next a natural geometric interpretation of the complex of mixed Hodge modules
{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}
resp.
{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{-\beta}
.

For this purpose, fix some value

\lambda\in W
. Then, by definition, we have
\lambda\in\Gamma(X,\mathscr{L})
, and interpreting this global section as a morphism
\lambda\colon X\hookrightarrow L
, we can consider the image
L_{\lambda}:=\im(\lambda)\subseteq L
. We identify the zero section of the projection
\pi\colon L\twoheadrightarrow X
inside
L
with
X
and recall that
L^{*}:=L\setminus X
denotes the complement of the zero section. We denote by
H_{\lambda}:=L_{\lambda}\cap X\subseteq X
the zero locus of the section
\lambda
(which was called
Z(\lambda)
in
1.2
) and by
U_{\lambda}:=X\setminus H_{\lambda}

its complement in
X
.

Notice that the full family of zero loci of sections of
L
is given by
\mathcal{Y}:=ev^{-1}(0)\to W
,
(s,\lambda)\mapsto\lambda
, i.e. the fibre of this map over a point
\lambda\in W
is exactly the hypersurface
H_{\lambda}
.
Similarly, we have

\mathcal{U}=(W\times X)\setminus\mathcal{Y}=\bigcup_{\lambda\in W}U_{\lambda}
, the evaluation morphism
ev
from Formula
(
6
) then restricts to a morphism

ev_{|\mathcal{U}}\colon\mathcal{U}\longrightarrow L^{*}.

Recall that we defined the constant (complex) pure Hodge module
{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}
in
2.2
. We then put

{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{\mathcal{U}}:={ev_{|\mathcal{U}}^{*}}\,{{}^{H\!}}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}[\dim W-1]\in\textup{HM}(\mathcal{U},{\mathds{C}}).

Moreover, for
\lambda\in W
as above, consider
the restriction
\lambda_{|U_{\lambda}}\colon U_{\lambda}\hookrightarrow L^{*}
. We put, for
\beta\in{\mathds{Q}}
,

{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{\lambda}:=\lambda^{*}_{|U_{\lambda}}\,{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}[-1]\in\textup{HM}(U_{\lambda},{\mathds{C}})

Proposition 3.9
.

We continue with the setup of
3.8
and additionally assume that
X
is projective. Let
\beta\in\mathds{C}
with
k\beta\in\mathds{Z}
. Then the following statements hold true:

1.

Let
a_{\mathcal{U}}\colon\mathcal{U}\rightarrow W
be the restriction of the projection
a_{W}\colon W\times X\rightarrow W
. Then we have an isomorphisms

a_{\mathcal{U},!}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{\mathcal{U}}\cong{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}\quad\quad\textup{and}\quad\quad a_{\mathcal{U},*}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{\mathcal{U}}(2\dim W+2\dim X)\cong{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{-\beta}

in
D^{b}\textup{MHM}(W,{\mathds{C}})
.

2.

For any
m\in{\mathds{N}}
, and any
\lambda\in W
we have isomorphisms of (complex) mixed Hodge structures

\begin{array}[]{rcl}H^{m}(i_{\lambda}^{*}\,{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}[-\dim W])&\cong&H_{c}^{\dim(X)+m}(U_{\lambda},\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{\lambda}),\\
\\
H^{m}(i_{\lambda}^{!}\,{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{-\beta}[\dim W])&\cong&H^{\dim(X)+m}(U_{\lambda},\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{\lambda})(\dim W+2\dim X).\\
\\
\end{array}

Proof.

In the course of the proof, we will make repeatedly use of the base change property for algebraic mixed Hodge modules, as stated in
[
Sai90
, Section 4.4.3]
.

1.

This is almost immediate by considering the following cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev_{|\mathcal{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{\mathcal{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{\mathcal{U}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ev}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{W}}

which yields (using (
8
))

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(*)}}{{\cong}}&a_{W,!}\,ev^{*}\,{j_{L,!}}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{L^{*}}[\dim W-1]\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(**)}}{{\cong}}&a_{W,!}\,j_{\mathcal{U},!}\,{ev_{|\mathcal{U}}^{*}}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{L^{*}}[\dim W-1]\\[5.0pt]
&=&a_{\mathcal{U},!}\,{ev_{|\mathcal{U}}^{*}}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{L^{*}}[\dim W-1]\\
&=&{a_{\mathcal{U},!}}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{\mathcal{U}},\end{array}

where the isomorphism
(*)
holds because
a_{W}
is proper (since
X
is projective)
and
ev
is smooth, and where
(**)
follows by base change. We then apply the duality functor
{\mathbb{D}}
on
D^{b}\textup{MHM}(W,{\mathds{C}})
on both sides of

a_{\mathcal{U},!}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{\mathcal{U}}\cong{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}
to obtain that
a_{\mathcal{U},*}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{\mathcal{U}}(2\dim X+2\dim W)\cong{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{-\beta}
.

2.

Write

{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{N}_{L}^{\beta}:=ev^{*}j_{L,!}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{L^{*}}[\dim W-1]\in\textup{MHM}(X\times W,{\mathds{C}}),

then by the proof of the previous

3.8
we have that

{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}\cong a_{W,!}\,{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{N}_{L}^{\beta}.

Now consider the cartesian diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\lambda}}\right\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\lambda}}\right\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\lambda}^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\lambda}}

Then

\begin{array}[]{rclcl}i_{\lambda}^{*}\,{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}&\cong&i_{\lambda}^{*}{a_{W,!}}\,{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{N}_{L}^{\beta}\\
\\
&\cong&a^{X}_{!}i^{X,*}_{\lambda}\,{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{N}_{L}^{\beta}=a^{X}_{!}\,i^{X,*}_{\lambda}ev^{*}j_{L,!}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{L^{*}}[\dim W-1]&&(\textup{base change})\\
\\
&\cong&a_{!}^{X}\,\lambda^{*}j_{L,!}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{L^{*}}[\dim W-1]&&(ev\circ i^{X}_{\lambda}=\lambda)\\
\\
&\cong&a_{*}^{X}\,\lambda^{*}j_{L,!}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{L^{*}}[\dim W-1]&&(a^{X}\textup{ proper}).\end{array}

Now we consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{*},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{|U_{\lambda}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{L}}

then base change yields
\lambda^{*}\,j_{L,!}\cong j_{!}\,\lambda_{|U_{\lambda}}^{*}
, so we get an isomorphism of objects in
D^{b}\textup{MHM}(\{\lambda\},{\mathds{C}})
(which we identify with the derived category of complex mixed Hodge structures).

\begin{array}[]{rl}i_{\lambda}^{*}\,{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}&\cong a_{*}^{X}\,j_{!}\,\lambda_{|U_{\lambda}}^{*}\,{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{L^{*}}[\dim W-1]\cong a_{*}^{X}\,j_{!}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{\lambda}[\dim W]\\
&\cong a_{!}^{X}\,j_{!}\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{\lambda}[\dim W]=a^{U_{\lambda}}_{!}\,{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{\lambda}[\dim W],\end{array}

where
a^{U_{\lambda}}\colon U_{\lambda}\twoheadrightarrow\{\lambda\}
and where we have used
a^{X}_{*}=a^{X}_{!}

since
X
is projective. We apply
H^{m}(-)
to both sides to obtain
an isomorphism of complex mixed Hodge structures

H^{m}(i_{\lambda}^{*}\,{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}[-\dim W])\cong H^{m}(a^{U_{\lambda}}_{!}\,{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{\lambda})=H^{m+\dim X}_{c}(U_{\lambda},{{}^{H\!}}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{\lambda}).

recall that we use the convention
{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{X}:=a_{X}^{*}{{}^{H}\!}{\mathds{C}}_{pt}[\dim(X)]
.

For the second statement, we apply the duality functor
{\mathbb{D}}
in
D^{b}\textup{MHM}(W,{\mathds{C}})
to the isomorphism

i_{\lambda}^{*}\,{{}^{H,*}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}\cong a^{U_{\lambda}}_{!}\,{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\beta}_{\lambda}[\dim W]
just proved, which gives

i_{\lambda}^{!}\,{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{-\beta}\cong a^{U_{\lambda}}_{*}\,{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{\lambda}(\dim W+2\dim X)[-\dim W],

and then by taking cohomology again we find that

H^{m}(i_{\lambda}^{!}\,{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{-\beta}[\dim W])\cong H^{\dim(X)+m}(U_{\lambda},\,^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{\lambda})(\dim W+2\dim X),

as required. ∎

In the subsequent sections of this article, we will investigate to which extent tautological systems for homogeneous spaces
X
are examples of the
\mathscr{D}
-modules underlying
{}^{H\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\beta}
for particular line bundles
L
and values
\beta
.

4
Non-vanishing criteria for tautological systems

The definition of a tautological system does not always describe a non-zero
\mathscr{D}
-module. In fact, for tautological systems arising from projective homogeneous spaces, this fails in a striking way, as we will see below in
Section

4.4
.
In that setup, tautological systems
\tau(\rho,\overline{Y},\beta)
