of) the Killing form, and
|\mu|=\sqrt{\langle\mu,\mu\rangle}
.

Corollary 5.2
.

If
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\neq 0
, then
\beta(\mathbf{e})
is a non-negative rational number.

Proof.

Since
\mu
is a highest weight, the inner product of
\mu
with all positive roots is a non-negative integer, hence
\langle\delta,\mu\rangle\geq 0
and so
\frac{2\langle\delta,\mu\rangle}{|\mu|^{2}}\in{\mathds{Q}}_{\geq 0}
.
∎

In the remainder of this section, we will give proof of
5.1
as well as a more geometric interpretation of it.

Notation 5.3
.

Throughout this section, we use the following notation.

•

\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}=\mathds{C}\mathbf{e}\oplus\mathfrak{g}
– the Lie algebra of
G^{\prime}

•

T
– a maximal torus of
G

•

B
– a Borel subgroup of
G
containing
T

•

\mathfrak{t}
– the Lie algebra of
T

•

\Phi(M,T)
– the roots of an (affine) algebraic group
M
relative to a subtorus
T
. This is the set of characters
\lambda\colon T\to\mathds{C}^{*}
of
T
such that

\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}\coloneqq\{\xi\in\mathfrak{m}\mid\operatorname{Ad}(t)\xi=\lambda(t)\xi\}\neq 0,

where
\mathfrak{m}
is the Lie algebra of
M
. (Cf.
[
Hum75
]
)

•

\Phi\coloneqq\Phi(G,T)
– the root system of
G
relative to
T
. As usual, we view this as a subset of
\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
.

•

\Phi^{+}\coloneqq\Phi(B,T)
– the choice of positive roots corresponding to
B

•

\Delta\subseteq\Phi^{+}
– the simple roots

•

\delta\coloneqq\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}\alpha
– the Weyl vector.

•

\mathsf{B}(-,-)
– the Killing form on
\mathfrak{g}

•

\langle-,-\rangle
– the symmetric bilinear form on
\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
induced by the restriction to
\mathfrak{t}
of the Killing form. Since
\mathfrak{g}
is semisimple,
\langle-,-\rangle
is nondegenerate.

Since
\mathfrak{g}
is semisimple, there is a decomposition

\mathfrak{g}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha}}}\right)\oplus\mathfrak{t}\oplus\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}}}\right).

Each
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
is one-dimensional. In fact, one can choose a generator
E_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
for each
\alpha\in\Phi
such that
[E_{\alpha},E_{-\alpha}]\eqqcolon H_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{t}
,
\mathsf{B}(E_{\alpha},E_{-\alpha})=1
,
[H_{\alpha},E_{\alpha}]=2E_{\alpha}
, and
[H_{\alpha},E_{-\alpha}]=-2E_{-\alpha}
. Note that the
H_{\alpha}
might not form a basis for
\mathfrak{t}
, as there may be too many of them.

A straightforward argument shows that for each
\alpha\in\Phi^{+}
,
H_{\alpha}
is the unique element of
\mathfrak{t}
for which
\mathsf{B}(H,H_{\alpha})=\alpha(H)
for all
H\in\mathfrak{t}
. We can use this property to define
H_{\lambda}
for all
\lambda\in\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
—then the nondegenerate bilinear form
\langle-,-\rangle
is given by

\langle\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\rangle=\mathsf{B}(H_{\lambda},H_{\lambda^{\prime}})=\lambda(H_{\lambda^{\prime}})=\lambda^{\prime}(H_{\lambda}).

Definition 5.4
.

The (second order)
Casimir element
is the element

C\coloneqq\sum_{i}A_{i}B_{i}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}),

where
\{A_{i}\}
is any basis for
\mathfrak{g}
, and
\{B_{i}\}
is the dual basis under the Killing form. In particular, if
\{H_{i}\}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathfrak{t}
with respect to the Killing form, then

C=\sum_{i}H_{i}^{2}+\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}E_{\alpha}E_{-\alpha}+\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}E_{-\alpha}E_{\alpha}.

A straightforward exercise
shows that
C
is in the center of
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
U
be an irreducible representation of
\mathfrak{g}
with
lowest
weight
\lambda
and
lowest
weight vector
v_{\lambda}
. Then

C\cdot v_{\lambda}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\lambda|^{2}-2\langle\delta,\lambda\rangle}}\right)v_{\lambda}.

Proof.

Then

\displaystyle C\cdot v_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i}H_{i}^{2}+\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}E_{\alpha}E_{-\alpha}+\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}E_{-\alpha}E_{\alpha}}}\right)\cdot v_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i}H_{i}^{2}-\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}H_{\alpha}+2\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}E_{\alpha}E_{-\alpha}}}\right)\cdot v_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i}H_{i}^{2}-2H_{\delta}}}\right)\cdot v_{\lambda},

since
E_{\alpha}
kills
v_{\lambda}
. Now use that
\sum_{i}H_{i}^{2}\cdot v_{\lambda}=\sum_{i}\lambda(H_{i})v_{i}=|\lambda|^{2}v_{i}
and
H_{\delta}\cdot v_{\lambda}=\lambda(H_{\delta})v_{\lambda}
.
∎

For the proof of
5.1
, we need
a few facts about differential operators on affine varieties, which we introduce now. The full power the theory of such operators is not needed here, so we only touch on a very small bit of it.

Set

R\coloneqq\Gamma(V,\mathcal{O}_{V}),\quad D_{V}\coloneqq\Gamma(V,\mathscr{D}_{V}),\quad\text{and}\quad S\coloneqq R/I,

where
I
is the defining ideal of
\hat{X}
.

Define

\begin{array}[]{rcl}A&\coloneqq&\{P\in D_{V}\mid P(I)\subseteq I\},\\
\\
J&\coloneqq&\{P\in D_{V}\mid P(R)\subseteq I\}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathds{N}^{n}}I\partial^{\alpha},\\
\end{array}

(15)

and

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\psi:A&\longrightarrow&\End_{\mathds{C}}(S)\\
P&\longmapsto&(\overline{f}\mapsto\overline{P\bullet f})\\
\\
D_{S}&\coloneqq&\operatorname{im}(\psi).\end{array}

(16)

Note that it is
not
immediately obvious that this
D_{S}
is the same as the ring of Grothendieck differential operators of
S
over
\mathds{C}
—that it is is the content of the surjectivity part of
[
Mil99
, 1.2. Prop.]
. That said, for our application in the proof of
5.1
, we will start with a particular element of
A
, and we will need to show that it is in fact in
J
. In other words, we need the following

Lemma 5.6

(
[
Mil99
, part of 1.2. Prop.]
)
.

\ker\psi=J
. In particular,
\ker\psi\subseteq ID_{V}
.

Under the assumption that
G
is semi-simple, the definition of
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
simplifies to

\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)=\mathscr{D}_{V}/\bigl(\mathscr{D}_{V}I+\mathscr{D}_{V}(Z(\xi)\mid\xi\in\mathfrak{g})+\mathscr{D}_{V}(Z(\mathbf{e})-\dim V+\beta(\mathbf{e}))\bigr).

Here, we denote by
Z(\xi)
the vector field
Z_{V}(\xi)
defined in
Section

4.1
and we will also denote by
Z
the map
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})\to D_{V}
extending it.

Because
X
is
G
-invariant, the ideal
I
is
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
-stable, i.e.
Z(\xi)(I)\subseteq I
for all
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
. Hence, the map
Z
induces a
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
-module structure on
S
for which the elements of
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
act via derivations. If
S_{d}
is the
d
th graded component of
S
, then

\xi\cdot S_{d}\subseteq S_{d}\quad\text{for all }\xi\in\mathfrak{g},

and

\mathbf{e}\cdot f=-df\quad\text{for all }f\in S_{d}.

(17)

Denote the induced map
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})\to\End_{\mathds{C}}(S)
by
Z_{S}
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Z_{S}(C)=Z_{S}(\mathbf{e})^{2}|\mu|^{2}-2Z_{S}(\mathbf{e})\langle\delta,\mu\rangle
.

Proof.

By definition,
R_{1}=V^{\vee}
. The construction of the embedding
X\hookrightarrow\mathds{P}V
implies that
R_{1}=S_{1}
also. Hence, if
x\in S_{1}\cong V^{\vee}
is a
lowest weight vector, it has
lowest weight
-\mu
(recall that
\mu
is the
highest
weight of
V
).
A straightforward argument shows that for all
d\in\mathbb{N}
, the element
x^{d}
is a
lowest weight vector of
S_{d}
with
lowest weight
-d\mu
.
Hence, by
5.5
,

C\cdot x^{d}=(|-d\mu|^{2}-2\langle\delta,-d\mu\rangle)x^{d}.

Since
C
is in the center of the universal enveloping algebra, it acts on the irreducible
\mathfrak{g}
-representation
S_{d}
as a scalar, which then must be the factor on the right hand side. Now use that
\mathbf{e}
acts on
S_{d}
as multiplication by
-d
(eq. (
17
)).
∎

By definition, the operators
Z(C)
and
Z(\mathbf{e})^{2}|\mu|^{2}-2Z(\mathbf{e})\langle\delta,\mu\rangle
are contained in the subalgebra
A
from (
15
). By
5.7
, their difference is in the kernel of the map
\psi
from (
16
). Hence, by
5.6
, we know that

Z(C)-\bigl(Z(\mathbf{e})^{2}|\mu|^{2}-2Z(\mathbf{e})\langle\delta,\mu\rangle\bigr)\in ID_{V}.

Applying the standard
D
-module transpose
(-)^{\top}
and identifying
Z(\mathbf{e})
with minus the Euler differential operator
1
1

1

The minus sign comes from the fact that the action of
\mathfrak{g}
on the coordinate ring of
V
is the contragredient action.

-E
gives

(Z(C))^{\top}-\bigl((E+\dim{V})^{2}|\mu|^{2}-2(E+\dim{V})\langle\delta,\mu\rangle\bigr)\in D_{V}I,

(18)

since
(-E)^{\top}=E+\dim V
and
I
is homogeneous.

Lemma 5.8
.

(Z(C))^{\top}=Z(C)
.

Proof.

Because
\mathfrak{g}
is semisimple,
[\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}]=\mathfrak{g}
. Hence,
\mathfrak{g}
acts on
V
via trace-free matrices. Let
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}
act on
V
via a square matrix
A=[a_{ij}]
. Then
Z(\xi)
is the derivation
-\sum_{i,j}a_{ji}x_{i}\partial_{x_{j}}
. The transpose of this operator is then
\sum a_{ji}\partial_{x_{j}}x_{i}=\sum a_{ji}x_{i}\partial_{x_{j}}+\Tr(A)=-Z(\xi)
. As
C
arises as evaluation on elements of
\mathfrak{g}
of a homogeneous quadric,
(Z(C))^{\top}=Z(C)
.
∎

Proof of
5.1
.

Combining
5.8
and eq. (
18
) yields

Z(C)-\bigl((E+\dim{V})^{2}|\mu|^{2}-2(E+\dim{V})\langle\delta,\mu\rangle\bigr)\in D_{V}I.

Taking cosets in
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
, we find

\displaystyle 0=\overline{Z(C)}

\displaystyle=\overline{(E+\dim{V})^{2}|\mu|^{2}-2(E+\dim{V})\langle\delta,\mu\rangle}

\displaystyle=\overline{(E+\dim{V})\bigl((E+\dim{V})|\mu|^{2}-2\langle\delta,\mu\rangle\bigr)}.

On the other hand, the defining ideal of
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
also contains

Z(\mathbf{e})-\dim{V}+\beta(\mathbf{e})=-E-\dim{V}+\beta(\mathbf{e})

So, we deduce

\beta(\mathbf{e})\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{0,\frac{2\langle\delta,\;\mu\rangle}{|\mu|^{2}}}}\right\}.

∎

5.2
Geometric interpretation

We now aim for the following geometric description of the quantity for
\beta(\mathbf{e})
from the previous section, showing the compatibility of the non-vanishing result of
{\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)_{|{V\setminus 0}}}
in
4.34
with that of

\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
in
5.1
.

Proposition 5.9
.

If
\mathscr{L}^{\otimes\ell}\cong\omega_{X}^{\otimes(-k)}
as
G
-equivariant line bundles for some integers
k,\ell\neq 0
, then

\frac{2\langle\delta,\mu\rangle}{\langle\mu,\mu\rangle}=\frac{\ell}{k}.

The proof of this proposition will be based on the correspondence between characters of
P
and equivariant line bundles on
G/P
(where
P
is parabolic), and identifying the character corresponding to the canonical bundle
\omega_{G/P}
(
5.12
below). For this, we first recall some facts about parabolic subgroups.

Recall (
[
Hum75
, §21.3]
) that a closed subgroup
P\leq G
is called
parabolic
if
G/P
is projective. We recall some facts about parabolic subgroups.

Lemma 5.10
.

NEWLINE

(a)

If
X
is a projective homogeneous
G
-space, then
X\cong G/P
for some parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
containing
B
.

(b)

There is an inclusion-preserving bijection between subsets
I\subseteq\Delta
and parabolic subgroups
P_{I}
containing
B
.
2
2

2

Although we won’t need it, the actual definition of
P_{I}
can be found directly above Th. 29.2 in
[
Hum75
]
.

(c)

\Phi(P_{I},T)=\Phi^{+}\cup(\Phi^{-}\cap\mathds{Z}I)
.

Proof.

(a)
This is standard. It uses that every parabolic subgroup contains a Borel subgroup (
[
Hum75
, Cor. 21.3.B.]
), and that all Borel subgroups are conjugate (
[
Hum75
, Th. 21.3]
).

(b)

[
Hum75
, Th. 29.3]
.

(c)

[
Hum75
, Th. 30.1]
.
∎

Let
P
be a parabolic subgroup of
G
containing a maximal torus
T
. Recall that the characters
\lambda\colon T\to\mathds{C}^{*}
which are extendable to
P
correspond one-to-one with
G
-equivariant line bundles
L_{\lambda,P}
on
G/P
; see, e.g.,
[
HTT08
, §9.11]
(although the argument there is for
P=B
, the same argument works verbatim with
N^{-}
replaced by the unipotent radical of the parabolic subgroup of
G
opposite
P
). Note that there are two common conventions for this correspondence—we choose the convention for which
P
acts on the fiber of
L_{\lambda,P}
at
P
as
b\cdot v=\lambda(b)v
.
3
3

3

The other convention is
b\cdot v=\lambda(b)^{-1}v
.
In this case, the sheaf of sections
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda,P}
of
L_{\lambda,P}
is given by

\Gamma(U,\mathscr{L}_{\lambda,P})=\{f\in\Gamma(q^{-1}(U),\mathscr{O}_{G})\mid f(gb)=\lambda(b)^{-1}f(g)\text{ for all }g\in G,b\in P\},

(19)

where
q\colon G\to G/P
is the quotient map. Since
L_{\lambda,P}
is
G
-equivariant, there is a
G
-equivariant structure on
\mathscr{L}_{\lambda,P}
. Although we won’t need to know this structure explicitly, it may help the reader to note that the induced action of
G
on
\Gamma(G/P,\mathscr{L}_{\lambda,P})
is given by

(g\cdot f)(g^{\prime})=f(g^{-1}g^{\prime})\qquad(g,g^{\prime}\in G,\;f\in\Gamma(G/P,\mathscr{L}_{\lambda})).

There are many proofs of the following theorem throughout the literature. It is often stated and proved only for
P=B
. However, it was originally proven for all parabolic subgroups, e.g. in
[
Ser54
]
or
[
Bot57
, Cor. to Th. V]
.

Theorem 5.11

(Borel–Weil)
.

If
-\lambda
is a dominant weight which is extendable to the parabolic subgroup
P
, then
\Gamma(G/P,\mathscr{L}_{\lambda,P})^{\vee}
is the irreducible representation of
G
with highest weight
-\lambda
.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
I\subseteq\Delta
be a subset of the set of simple roots, and let
P_{I}
be the corresponding parabolic subgroup. Define

\delta_{I}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\setminus\mathds{Z}I}\alpha.

Then

\omega_{G/P_{I}}\cong\mathscr{L}_{2\delta_{I},P_{I}}.

Proof.

The following argument is based on the argument given in the MathOverflow post
[
Sco
]
.

According to
[
CG10
, Lem. 1.4.9]
,
T^{*}(G/P_{I})
is the (unique)
G
-equivariant vector bundle on
G/P_{I}
whose fiber over
P_{I}
is the
P_{I}
-module

\mathfrak{p}_{I}^{\perp}\coloneqq\{\xi\in\mathfrak{g}\mid\langle\xi,x\rangle=0\text{ for all }x\in\mathfrak{p}_{I}\},

where
P_{I}
acts via the coadjoint action.
But, letting
T
be a maximal torus of
G
contained in
P_{I}
, we have a sequence of
P_{I}
-isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{I}^{\perp}

\displaystyle\cong(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{p}_{I})^{\vee}

\displaystyle\cong\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\Phi\text{ not a root}\\
\text{of }P_{I}\text{ rel.~to }T\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}}}\right)^{\vee}

\displaystyle\cong\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in-(\Phi^{+}\setminus\mathds{Z}I)}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}}}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\setminus\mathds{Z}I}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}.

Taking the determinant gives the
P_{I}
-equivariant line bundle whose fiber at
P_{I}
is the
P_{I}
-module

\bigotimes_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\setminus\mathds{Z}I}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}.

The action of
P_{I}
on this module is determined by the action of
T
, and the action of
t\in T
is just multiplication by

\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\setminus\mathds{Z}I}\alpha(t)=2\delta_{I}(t).

Thus,
\omega_{G/P_{I}}\cong\mathscr{L}_{2\delta_{I},P_{I}}
.
∎

We need one more technical lemma before beginning the proof of
5.9
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
I\subseteq\Delta
be a subset of the simple roots. Then
\langle\delta,\delta_{I}\rangle=\langle\delta_{I},\delta_{I}\rangle
.

Proof.

Set
\delta_{I}^{\prime}\coloneqq\delta-\delta_{I}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}\cap\mathds{Z}I}\alpha
. We want to show that
\langle\delta^{\prime}_{I},\delta_{I}\rangle=0
. To begin with, let
\Phi_{I}
be
\Phi\cap\mathds{R}I
viewed as a subset of the vector space
\mathds{R}I
. It is immediately clear that
\Phi_{I}
is a root system (in
\mathds{R}I
), and that
I
forms a base of
\Phi_{I}
. Hence,
\delta^{\prime}_{I}
is the Weyl vector
\delta_{\Phi_{I}}
of
\Phi_{I}
(with respect to this base). Moreover, the inner product of two elements of
\mathds{R}I
is the same as in the ambient vector space
\mathfrak{t}^{\vee}
of the root system
\Phi
. So, the coroots of
\Phi_{I}
are the coroots
H_{\alpha}\coloneqq 2\alpha/\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle
of
\Phi
for
\alpha\in\Phi_{I}
. Therefore, by
[
Hal15
, Prop. 8.38]
,

\langle\delta^{\prime}_{I},H_{\alpha}\rangle=\langle\delta_{\Phi_{I}},H_{\alpha}\rangle=1

for all
\alpha\in I
. Hence, for all
\alpha\in I
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{2\langle\alpha,\delta_{I}\rangle}{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}

\displaystyle=\langle H_{\alpha},\delta_{I}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle H_{\alpha},\delta-\delta^{\prime}_{I}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle H_{\alpha},\delta\rangle-\langle H_{\alpha},\delta^{\prime}_{I}\rangle

\displaystyle=1-1=0,

where the final equality again uses
[
Hal15
, Prop. 8.38]
. Therefore,
\langle\alpha,\delta_{I}\rangle=0
for all
\alpha\in I
and hence for all
\alpha\in\mathds{R}I
. In particular,
\langle\delta^{\prime}_{I},\delta_{I}\rangle=0
.
∎

Proof of
5.9
.

Since
X
is a projective
G
-homogeneous space, it is isomorphic by
5.10
to
G/P_{I}
for some
I
. By assumption,
\mathscr{L}^{\otimes\ell}\cong\omega_{X}^{\otimes(-k)}
as
G
-equivariant line bundles. Therefore, since
\Pic(X)
and therefore
\Pic^{G}(X)
is torsion-free, and applying
5.12
,

\mathscr{L}\cong\mathscr{L}_{-\frac{k}{\ell}2\delta_{I},P}.

Therefore, by Borel–Weil (
5.11
), the
G
-representation
V=\Gamma(X,\mathscr{L})^{\vee}
has highest weight

\mu=-(-\frac{2k}{\ell}\delta_{I})=\frac{2k}{\ell}\delta_{I}.

Then

\displaystyle\frac{2\langle\delta,\mu\rangle}{\langle\mu,\mu\rangle}

\displaystyle=\frac{\ell\langle\delta,\delta_{I}\rangle}{k\langle\delta_{I},\delta_{I}\rangle}=\frac{\ell\langle\delta_{I},\delta_{I}\rangle}{k\langle\delta_{I},\delta_{I}\rangle}=\frac{\ell}{k}=\beta(\mathbf{e}).\qed

We finish this section with the following well-known fact concering the anticanonical bundle of
X=G/P
the proof of which we include for the convenience of the reader.

Lemma 5.14
.

Assume only that
G
is reductive. Then
X=G/P
is a Fano variety.

Proof.

By
[
Jan03
, II.4.4]
, a
G
-equivariant line bundle
\mathscr{L}_{-\lambda,P}
on
G/P
is ample if and only if

\langle\lambda,\alpha\rangle>0\qquad\text{for all $\alpha\in\Delta\setminus I$}.

By
5.12
, the anticanonical bundle
\omega_{X}^{\vee}
is
\mathscr{L}_{-2\delta_{I},P}
, so we need to check that
\langle\delta_{I},\alpha\rangle>0
holds for any
\alpha\in\Delta\setminus I
. The reflection
s_{\alpha}\colon\Phi\to\Phi
given by

s_{\alpha}(\beta)=\beta-\frac{2\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle}{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}\alpha

maps
\alpha
to
-\alpha
and permutes
\Phi^{+}\setminus(\mathds{Z}I\cup\{\alpha\})
(this follows easily from the defining property of the set of simple roots
\Delta
). Hence,
s_{\alpha}(\delta_{I})=\delta_{I}-\alpha
, which means that
\frac{2\langle\alpha,\delta_{I}\rangle}{\langle\alpha,\alpha\rangle}=1>0
.
∎

6
Tautological systems associated to homogeneous spaces

The purpose of this section is to gather all the previous results and to apply them to the special case where we are given a projective variety
X
with a transitive group action together with a very ample equivariant line bundle.
We obtain a representation on the space of sections, and we can therefore consider the corresponding tautological system. The non-vanishing results from
Section

4
then apply. Moreover, we show in
Section

6.1
a localization property of the corresponding Fourier transform
\hat{\tau}
, and in
Section

6.2
a property that is in a certain sense dual to the first one, which is why we called “colocalization property”. These results, combined with the discussion in
Section

3
will finally give our main result
(
6.14
) stating that the tautological system
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
, if non-zero, underlies a complex pure resp. a mixed Hodge module, this is discussed in
Section

6.3
.

6.1
Localization property of
\widehat{\tau}

The purpose of this and of the following section is to prove a key property of the differential system
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
concerning its relation to its restriction
i^{+}\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
, where
i\colon\{0\}\hookrightarrow V
. In this section we only consider the case where
\beta(\mathbf{e})\in{\mathds{C}}\setminus{\mathds{Z}}
, whereas in the next section also the case where
\beta(\mathbf{e})\in{\mathds{Z}}
is studied. For the moment, we are working in a slightly more general setup, therefore, we let temporarily
V
be any finite-dimensional vector space, and we consider the Euler operator
E
on
V
(i.e. the differential of the scaling action). For
\lambda\in\mathds{C}
, define
\mathbf{Eig}(V,\lambda)
to be the full subcategory of
\operatorname{Mod}(\Gamma(V,\mathscr{D}_{V}))
consisting of modules
M
satisfying

M=\bigoplus_{\mu\in\lambda+\mathds{Z}}M_{\mu},

(20)

where
M_{\mu}\coloneqq\ker(E-\mu)\subseteq M
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\mathscr{M}\in\operatorname{Mod}_{qc}(\mathscr{D}_{V})
. If
\Gamma(V,\mathscr{M})\in\mathbf{Eig}(V,\lambda)
for some
\lambda\in\mathds{C}\setminus\mathds{Z}
, then

\mathscr{M}\cong j_{+}j^{+}\mathscr{M},

where
j
denotes the open embedding of
V\setminus\{0\}
into
V
.

Proof.

Let
N:=\dim V
and choose coordinates
x_{1},\dots,x_{N}
on
V
. The distinguished triangle

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\{0\}}(\mathscr{M})\to\mathscr{M}\to j_{+}j^{+}\mathscr{M}\xrightarrow{+1}

in
D_{qc}^{b}(\mathscr{D}_{V})
(see
[
HTT08
, Prop. 1.7.1(i)]
) shows that it suffices to prove
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\{0\}}(\mathscr{M})=0
in order to conclude the claim. Since
V
is affine and
\mathscr{M}
is quasi-coherent, we actually just need to show

H^{i}_{\mathfrak{m}}(M)=0\quad\text{for all }i,

where
M=\Gamma(V,\mathscr{M})
and
\mathfrak{m}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
.

Recall that
H^{i}_{\mathfrak{m}}(M)
may be computed as the cohomology of the Čech complex

0\to M\to\bigoplus_{i}M_{x_{i}}\to\bigoplus_{i,j}M_{x_{i}x_{j}}\to\cdots\to M_{x_{1}\cdots x_{N}}\to 0.

A straightforward application of the definition of eigenvector implies (a) that each term in this complex is also in
\mathbf{Eig}(V,\lambda)
, and (b) that
\mathbf{Eig}(V,\lambda)
is closed under taking subquotients. Hence, each
H^{i}_{\mathfrak{m}}(M)
is in
\mathbf{Eig}(V,\lambda)
.

Since
H^{i}_{\mathfrak{m}}(M)
is
\mathfrak{m}
-torsion, it remains to show that every
\mathfrak{m}
-torsion module in
\mathbf{Eig}(V,\lambda)
is zero. Let
M^{\prime}
be one such module, and assume there is a nonzero
n\in M^{\prime}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
n\in M^{\prime}_{\mu}
for some
\mu\in\lambda+\mathds{Z}
and that
\mathfrak{m}n=0
. Then

\mu n=E\cdot n=\sum_{i=0}^{N}x_{i}\partial_{i}\cdot n=\sum_{i=0}^{N}(\partial_{i}x_{i}-1)\cdot n=-Nn.

Thus, because
n\neq 0
,
\alpha
must be
-N
—in particular,
\alpha
, and therefore also
\lambda
, must be an integer, which is false by assumption. Hence,
M^{\prime}=0
.
∎

We draw a conclusion of the previous general result that concerns the Fourier transform of tautological systems as studied in
4.11
, where we only make the assumption that the boundary of the
G^{\prime}
-orbit is reduced to the origin in the vector space
V
. This is of course satisfied in the case of interest like in the situation studied in
4.34
.

Corollary 6.2
.

Let
\rho\colon G^{\prime}\to\GL(V)
be a finite-dimensional rational representation of an algebraic group of the form
G^{\prime}=G\times\mathds{C}^{*}
, where
\mathds{C}^{*}
acts by scaling elements of
V
. Let
Y\subseteq V
be a
G^{\prime}
-orbit and let
\overline{Y}
be its closure. Assume that
\overline{Y}\setminus Y=\{0\}
.
Let
\beta\colon\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\to\mathds{C}
be a Lie algebra homomorphism with
\beta(\mathbf{e})\in\mathds{C}\setminus\mathds{Z}
. Then the
\mathscr{D}_{V}
-module
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\overline{Y},\beta)
from
1.1
satisfies

\hat{\tau}(\rho,\overline{Y},\beta)\cong j_{+}j^{+}\hat{\tau}(\rho,\overline{Y},\beta),

where
j
denotes the open embedding of
V\setminus\{0\}
into
V
.

Proof.

By
6.1
, it suffices to prove that
\Gamma(V,\hat{\tau}(\rho,\overline{Y},\beta))\in\mathbf{Eig}(V,\beta(\mathbf{e}))
. To do this, let

P=\sum_{\alpha\gamma}c_{\alpha\gamma}x^{\alpha}\partial^{\gamma}

be a global section of
\mathscr{D}_{V}
. In
\Gamma(V,\hat{\tau}(\rho,\overline{Y},\beta))
, we have

\displaystyle EP

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\gamma}c_{\alpha\gamma}(|\alpha|-|\gamma|)x^{\alpha}\partial^{\gamma}+\sum_{\alpha\gamma}c_{\alpha\gamma}x^{\alpha}\partial^{\gamma}E

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\gamma}c_{\alpha\gamma}(|\alpha|-|\gamma|)x^{\alpha}\partial^{\gamma}+\sum_{\alpha\gamma}\beta(\mathbf{e})c_{\alpha\gamma}x^{\alpha}\partial^{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\gamma}(\beta(\mathbf{e})+|\alpha|-|\gamma|)c_{\alpha\gamma}x^{\alpha}\partial^{\gamma}

\displaystyle\in\bigoplus_{\mu\in\beta(\mathbf{e})+\mathds{Z}}\hat{\tau}_{\mu}.

Thus,
\Gamma(V,\hat{\tau}(\rho,\overline{Y},\beta))\in\mathbf{Eig}(V,\beta(\mathbf{e}))
.
∎

6.2
Colocalization property of
\widehat{\tau}

In this section, we consider a similar property as just studied, but which also includes the case where
\beta(\mathbf{e})\in{\mathds{Z}}
.
It turns out (see
example

6.4
below) that in general the
\mathscr{D}_{V}
-module
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
is not equal to the direct image of its restriction to
V\setminus{0}
, but to one cohomology group of the properly supported direct image. In the case where the value of
\beta
on
\mathbf{e}
is not an integer, this is consistent with the previous result since both direct images are equal then.

We work in the setup described before
4.34
, i.e.
X\subseteq{\mathds{P}}V
is projective with affine cone
\hat{X}
with vanishing ideal
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{V}
. Consider the embeddings

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\setminus\{0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{0\}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\{0\}}}

Our main result in this section is the following.

Theorem 6.3
.

If
\beta(\mathbf{e})\notin{\mathds{Z}}_{\leq 0}
, then

\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
is colocalized, in the sense that the canonical morphism

H^{0}j_{\dagger}j^{+}\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\longrightarrow\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)

is an isomorphism in
\textup{Mod}_{h}(\mathscr{D}_{V})
.

Before we discuss the proof of this theorem we show by example that integral parameters may correspond to systems that are colocalized but not localized. From here on and until the end of this paragraph, in order to keep the notation light, we write
\hat{\tau}
for the
\mathscr{D}_{V}
-module

\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
that appears in the theorem above.

Example 6.4
.

Let
X
be
{\mathds{P}}^{1}\times{\mathds{P}}^{1}
, where the group
G:=\SL_{2}\times\SL_{2}
acts transitively via the action on each factor. Choose the projective embedding induced by the line bundle
\mathcal{O}(1,1)
. The target space is
{\mathds{P}}V={\mathds{P}}^{3}
and
X
is cut out by
f=x_{1,1}x_{2,2}-x_{2,1}x_{1,2}
. We write

E=x_{1,1}\partial_{1,1}+x_{1,2}\partial_{1,2}+x_{2,1}\partial_{2,1}+x_{2,2}\partial_{2,2}
.
The interesting
\beta(\mathbf{e})
(for which, according to
4.34
, the restriction of
\hat{\tau}
to
V\setminus\{0\}
is non-zero and has full support) equals
6

(so that
\beta^{\prime}(\mathbf{e})=\trace(E)-\beta(\mathbf{e})=-2
), and then the defining ideal of
\hat{\tau}
is generated by
f
,
E-\beta^{\prime}(\mathbf{e})=E+2
, and the operators

\displaystyle x_{2,1}\partial_{1,1}+x_{2,2}\partial_{1,2},\quad x_{1,1}\partial_{2,1}+x_{1,2}\partial_{2,2},\quad x_{1,1}\partial_{1,2}+x_{2,1}\partial_{2,2},\quad x_{1,2}\partial_{1,1}+x_{2,2}\partial_{2,1},

\displaystyle\theta_{1,1}+\theta_{1,2}+1,\quad\theta_{2,1}+\theta_{2,2}+1,\quad\theta_{1,1}+\theta_{2,1}+1,\quad\theta_{1,2}+\theta_{2,2}+1,

where we write
\theta_{i,j}
for
x_{i,j}\partial_{i,j}
.

Let
P=\partial_{1,1}\partial_{2,2}-\partial_{2,1}\partial_{1,2}
. It is an easy calculation using the above generators to see that the class of
x_{i,j}P
is zero in
\hat{\tau}
, for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
. A computer computation shows that
P
is not zero in
\hat{\tau}
, and so
\hat{\tau}
contains a submodule
\mathcal{K}
of holonomic length one that is supported at the origin. In particular, we certainly have
\hat{\tau}\neq j_{+}j^{+}\hat{\tau}
in this case.

Inspection shows that there is a natural
\mathscr{D}_{V}
-module map from
\hat{\tau}
to the local cohomology sheaf
\mathcal{H}=H^{1}_{\hat{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{V})
that sends the coset of
1
to the coset of
1/f
. Notice that this map is not surjective, since
\mathcal{H}\cong\mathcal{O}_{V}(*\hat{X})/\mathcal{O}_{V}
is generated by
1/f^{2}
, due to the fact that the Bernstein-Sato polynomial of
f
is
(s+1)(s+2)
.

The image of

\hat{\tau}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
is the Kashiwara–Brylinski module
\mathcal{B}
attached to

f
(i.e. the module obtained as
\hat{\iota}_{{\dagger}+}\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}\setminus\{0\}}\in\textup{Mod}(\mathscr{D}_{V})
, recall that

\hat{\iota}\colon L^{\vee,*}\cong\hat{X}\setminus\{0\}\hookrightarrow V
is the composition of the closed embedding
i\colon\hat{X}\setminus\{0\}\hookrightarrow V\setminus\{0\}
with the canonical open embedding
j\colon V\setminus\{0\}\hookrightarrow V
from above), and so
\mathcal{B}
is in particular simple and self-dual. The cokernel
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{H}/\mathcal{B}
is the
\mathscr{D}_{V}
-module generated by
1/f^{2}
; it is supported at the origin and of holonomic length one. The kernel is the module
\mathcal{K}
above.
We thus arrive at the following sequence of
\mathscr{D}_{V}
-modules.

0\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}\longrightarrow\hat{\tau}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow 0.

It is automatic that
{\mathbb{D}}\mathcal{K}\cong\mathcal{C}
since both are length one and supported at the origin, but one can also verify that
{\mathbb{D}}\mathcal{H}\cong\hat{\tau}
. Moreover, it follows from the fact that
\mathcal{O}_{V}(*\hat{X})
is localized along
\hat{X}
that it is also localized at
\{0\}
, i.e. that we have
j_{+}j^{+}\mathcal{O}_{V}(*\hat{X})=\mathcal{O}_{V}(*\hat{X})
. Then since
j_{+}j^{+}\mathcal{O}_{V}=\mathcal{O}_{V}
we get that
j_{+}j^{+}\mathcal{H}\cong\mathcal{H}
, and thus the module
{\mathbb{D}}\hat{\tau}
also satisfies

j_{+}j^{+}{\mathbb{D}}\hat{\tau}\cong{\mathbb{D}}\hat{\tau}.

The proof of
6.3
will be given after several intermediate steps. First we recall that we have the algebra

\mathscr{A}_{V}
(see
4.14
), which is the universal enveloping algebra of the Lie algebroid
\mathcal{O}_{V}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
. It comes with a (in general non-surjective) algebra homomorphism
\widetilde{Z}_{V}\colon\mathscr{A}_{V}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}_{V}
which extends the map
Z_{V}
as defined
in
4.1
. Then we consider the left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-module

\hat{\tau}^{\mathscr{A}}:=\mathscr{A}_{V}/\mathscr{A}_{V}I+\mathscr{A}_{V}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\xi-\beta^{\prime}(\xi)\,|\,\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}}\right).

From the right exactness of the tensor product we get

\hat{\tau}=\mathscr{D}_{V}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}\hat{\tau}^{\mathscr{A}}=H^{0}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathscr{D}_{V}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}\hat{\tau}^{\mathscr{A}}}}\right),

using that
\widetilde{Z}_{V}
makes
\mathscr{D}_{V}
into a right
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-module.
We first have the following comparison result.

Lemma 6.5
.

If
H^{k}(\omega_{V}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}\hat{\tau}^{\mathscr{A}})=0
for
k=0,-1
, then also
H^{k}(\DR\hat{\tau})=H^{k}(\omega_{V}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{D}_{V}}\hat{\tau})=0
for
k=0,-1.

Proof.

Consider the Grothendieck spectral sequence for the composition of functors

\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathscr{D}_{V}}-
and
\mathscr{D}_{V}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}-
, with
E_{2}
-term

E_{2}^{p,q}=H^{p}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathscr{D}_{V}}^{\mathbb{L}}H^{q}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathscr{D}_{V}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}\hat{\tau}^{\mathscr{A}}}}\right)}}\right)\Longrightarrow H^{p+q}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\omega_{V}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}\hat{\tau}^{\mathscr{A}}}}\right).

We clearly have that
E_{2}^{0,0}=H^{0}(\omega_{V}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}\hat{\tau}^{\mathscr{A}})
and moreover, because we are dealing with the second page of a third quadrant spectral sequence,
E_{2}^{-1,0}
injects into

H^{-1}(\omega_{V}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}\hat{\tau}^{\mathscr{A}})
. Hence, under the assumption of the lemma, we obtain

E_{2}^{0,0}=H^{0}(\omega_{V}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{D}_{V}}\hat{\tau})=0\quad\quad\textup{and}\quad\quad E_{2}^{-1,0}=H^{-1}(\omega_{V}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{D}_{V}}\hat{\tau})=0.\qed

Next consider the following adjuction triangle

j_{\dagger}j^{+}\hat{\tau}\longrightarrow\hat{\tau}\longrightarrow(i_{\{0\},+}i_{\{0\}}^{\dagger})\hat{\tau}[\dim V]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\longrightarrow}}

(21)

and the associated exact sequence

0\longrightarrow H^{-1}((i_{\{0\},+}i_{\{0\}}^{\dagger})\hat{\tau}[\dim V])\longrightarrow H^{0}(j_{\dagger}j^{+}\hat{\tau})\longrightarrow\hat{\tau}\longrightarrow H^{0}((i_{\{0\},+}i_{\{0\}}^{\dagger})\hat{\tau}[\dim V])

We would like to show that the left- and the rightmost terms in this sequence vanish.
Since clearly
i_{\{0\},+}
is an exact functor, it suffice to show that

H^{k}(i_{\{0\}}^{\dagger}\hat{\tau}[\dim V])=0

for
k=0,-1
. To that end, we apply the functor
a_{V,+}
(where

a_{V}\colon V\rightarrow\{pt\}
is the projection) to the triangle
(
21
), this yields

a_{V,+}j_{\dagger}j^{+}\hat{\tau}\longrightarrow a_{V,+}\hat{\tau}\longrightarrow i_{\{0\}}^{\dagger}\hat{\tau}[\dim V]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\longrightarrow}}

(22)

since

a_{V,+}i_{\{0\},+}i_{\{0\}}^{\dagger}\hat{\tau}[\dim V]\cong(a_{V}\circ i_{\{0\}})_{+}i_{\{0\}}^{\dagger}\hat{\tau}[\dim V]\cong a_{\{0\},+}i_{\{0\}}^{\dagger}\hat{\tau}[\dim V]\cong i_{\{0\}}^{\dagger}\hat{\tau}[\dim V]

as elements in
D^{b}({\mathds{C}})
.

Now we have the following piece of the associated cohomology sequence of the triangle (
22
)

H^{-1}a_{V,+}\hat{\tau}\longrightarrow H^{-1}i_{\{0\}}^{\dagger}\hat{\tau}[\dim V]\longrightarrow H^{0}a_{V,+}j_{\dagger}j^{+}\hat{\tau}\longrightarrow H^{0}a_{V,+}\hat{\tau}\longrightarrow H^{0}i_{\{0\}}^{\dagger}\hat{\tau}[\dim V]\longrightarrow 0.

(23)

Here zero on the right most term comes from the vanishing

H^{1}a_{V,+}j_{\dagger}j^{+}\hat{\tau}=0,

which holds since both functors
a_{V,+}
and
j_{\dagger}
are right exact.
We now claim

Lemma 6.6
.

The map

H^{-1}a_{V,+}\hat{\tau}\longrightarrow H^{-1}i_{\{0\}}^{\dagger}\hat{\tau}[\dim V]

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

It can be shown more generally that under the assumption made here, we have an isomorphism

a_{V,+}\hat{\tau}\longrightarrow i_{\{0\}}^{\dagger}\hat{\tau}[\dim V]

in
D^{b}({\mathds{C}})
. In order to see this, we apply
[
Ste19
, Lemma 4.4]
(which is based on an earlier result in
[
RW19
, Lemma 3.3]
),
when seeing
a_{V}\colon V\rightarrow\{pt\}
as a bundle over the point
\{pt\}
. Then it is clear that this map is fibered in the sense of
[
Ste19
, Definition 4.1]
. It therefore remains to check that the
\mathscr{D}_{V}
-module
\hat{\tau}
is twistedly
{\mathds{C}}^{*}
-quasi-equivariant (as defined in
[
Ste19
, Definition 4.2]
). This is a condition that depends only on the restriction

j^{+}\hat{\tau}
, and this restriction has support on
\hat{X}\setminus\{0\}
.
Recall that we have the isomorphism
L^{*}\cong\hat{X}\setminus\{0\}
, obtained from composing the restriction to
L^{\vee,*}
of the blow-up
L^{\vee}\rightarrow\hat{X}
with the fiberwise isomorphism
\textup{inv}\colon L^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\rightarrow}}L^{\vee,*}
.
It is therefore sufficient
to show that
\iota^{+}\hat{\tau}
is
twistedly
{\mathds{C}}^{*}
-quasi-equivariant with respect to the

{\mathds{C}}^{*}
-action in the fibres of
L\rightarrow X
, where
\iota
is the composition of
j\colon V\setminus\{0\}\hookrightarrow V
with the closed embedding
\hat{X}\setminus\{0\}\hookrightarrow V\setminus\{0\}
and with the above isomorphism
L^{*}\cong\hat{X}\setminus\{0\}
.

It follows from
4.34
that this restriction is either zero, in which case the equivariance property we are after is trivially satisfied, or else equals
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k}
. It is an easy exercise to check (e.g., locally over trivializing neighborhoods) that
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k}
is
twistedly
{\mathds{C}}^{*}
-quasi-equivariant.
∎

By using the exact sequence (
23
) as well as the previous lemma,
6.3
is proved once we have shown that
H^{k}(a_{V,+}\hat{\tau})=0
for
k=0,-1
. But clearly
a_{V,+}\hat{\tau}=a_{V,*}\DR(\hat{\tau})
since
a_{V}
is an affine morphism. Therefore, by
6.5
, we are left to show the following.

Proposition 6.7
.

Using the above notation, we have

H^{k}(\omega_{V}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}\hat{\tau}^{\mathscr{A}})=0

for
k=0,-1
.

For this, we will need some further preparations. We start with an algebraic property of the left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-module

\mathscr{A}_{V}/\mathscr{A}_{V}\mathcal{I}
.

Lemma 6.8
.

1.

\mathcal{I}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{V}
has naturally the structure of a left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-module (and consequently, also
\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}
has)

2.

For any
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
, we have

\mathscr{A}_{V}\cdot\mathcal{I}\cdot\xi\subseteq\mathscr{A}_{V}\cdot\mathcal{I}

as subsets of
\mathscr{A}_{V}
. Consequently,
\mathscr{A}_{V}\cdot\mathcal{I}
is a two-sided ideal, and
\mathscr{A}_{V}/\mathscr{A}_{V}\mathcal{I}

is also a right
\mathscr{A}
-module (i.e., it is sheaf of rings).

Proof.

1.

Clearly,
\mathcal{O}_{V}
is a left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-module
through
\tilde{Z}_{V}\colon\mathscr{A}_{V}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}_{V}
. We need to show that this left action leaves
\mathcal{I}
invariant. Let
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
and let
g\in\mathcal{I}
be given.
Consider the following piece
of the dual to the conormal sequence of
\hat{X}\subseteq V

0\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}\!}er_{{\mathds{C}}}(\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}})\longrightarrow{\mathscr{D}\!er}_{{\mathds{C}}}(\mathcal{O}_{V})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\longrightarrow}}{H\!}om_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}),

Since
\hat{X}\subseteq V
is a
G^{\prime}
-variety,
Z_{V}(\xi)
descends to a derivation of
\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}
, i.e., it lies in the kernel of the map
\alpha
. Therefore
Z_{V}(\xi)(g)\in\mathcal{I}
.

2.

Since
\mathscr{A}_{V}
is the universal envelopping algebra of the Lie algebroid
\mathcal{O}_{V}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
, for any
g\in\mathcal{O}_{V}
, the commutator

\xi\cdot g-g\cdot\xi

must be equal to the result of applying the anchor map to
\xi
, and then applying the correspondig derivation to
g
. But the anchor map
\mathcal{O}_{V}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\rightarrow\Theta_{V}
is nothing but the scalar extension of
Z_{V}
, so that
\xi\cdot g-g\cdot\xi=Z_{V}(\xi)(g)
, which lies in
\mathcal{I}
by point 1. Consequently

g\cdot\xi=\xi\cdot g-Z_{V}(\xi)(g)\in\mathscr{A}_{V}\cdot\mathcal{I}

for
g\in\mathcal{I}
, as required.

∎

We next consider a homological construction that can be considered as a generalization of both the Spencer complex in
\mathscr{D}
-module theory (see, e.g.
[
HTT08
, Lemma 1.5.27.]
) and of the Euler-Koszul complex as defined in the theory of hypergeometric differential systems (
[
MMW05
, Section 4]
) and which is closely related to Lie algebra cohomology resp. homology (see, e.g.,
[
HS97
, Section VII.4]
). We therefore call it the Euler-Koszul-Chevalley-Eilenberg-Spencer complex. Let first
\mathcal{N}
be a right
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-module. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{-\ell}(\mathcal{N}):=\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\bigwedge_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}^{\ell}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathcal{O}_{V}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}}}\right)=\mathcal{N}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\bigwedge^{\ell}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},

where the differential is as follows

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\delta^{-\ell}\colon\mathcal{S}^{-\ell}(\mathcal{N})&\longrightarrow&\mathcal{S}^{-\ell+1}(\mathcal{N})\\
\\
m\otimes(\xi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\xi_{\ell})&\longmapsto&\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}(-1)^{i-1}m(\xi_{i}-\beta^{\prime}(\xi_{i}))\otimes(\xi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{\xi}_{i}\wedge\ldots\wedge\xi_{\ell})+\\
&&\sum\limits_{1\leq i<j\leq\ell}(-1)^{i+j}m\otimes([\xi_{i},\xi_{j}]\wedge\xi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{\xi}_{i}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{\xi}_{j}\wedge\ldots\wedge\xi_{\ell}).\end{array}

where the right
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-module structure on
\mathcal{N}
is used in the first term of the differential when writing
m(\xi_{i}-\beta^{\prime}(\xi_{i}))
.
In general,
\mathcal{S}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{N})
will be a complex of sheaves of
{\mathds{C}}
-vector spaces only.

We will apply this construction several times, but in particular in the following more special situation.
Let
\mathcal{M}
be a left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-module (e.g.
\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}
). Consider the sheaf

\mathscr{A}_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{M}.

We view this sheaf as an
(\mathscr{A}_{V},\mathscr{A}_{V})
-bimodule as follows: The left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-action is given by

b(a\otimes m)=ba\otimes m.

The right action is induced by

\displaystyle(a\otimes m)f

\displaystyle=af\otimes m\qquad(f\in\mathcal{O}_{V})

\displaystyle(a\otimes m)\xi

\displaystyle=a\xi\otimes m-a\otimes\xi m\qquad(\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}).

It is easy to check that this construction extends to a functor from left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-modules to
(\mathscr{A}_{V},\mathscr{A}_{V})
-bimodules.

To consider a specific example, we can take
\mathcal{M}:=\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}
, which is a left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-module by
6.8
above.
Let
\psi\colon\mathscr{A}_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}\to\mathscr{A}_{V}/\mathscr{A}_{V}\mathcal{I}
,
a\otimes\overline{g}\mapsto\overline{a\cdot g}
be the canonical isomorphism of left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-modules. Now by the previous construction, the left hand side is also a right
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-module, and by invoking
6.8
again, so is the right hand side. Then the morphism is also an isomorphism of right
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-modules: Since
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
kills the element 1 of
\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}
, we have (for
a\in\mathscr{A}_{V}
,
\overline{g}\in\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}
and
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
)

\displaystyle\psi((a\otimes\overline{g})\xi)

\displaystyle=\psi((ag\otimes 1)\xi)

\displaystyle=\psi(ag\xi\otimes 1-ag\otimes(\xi\cdot 1))

\displaystyle=\psi(ag\xi\otimes 1)

\displaystyle=\overline{ag\xi}

\displaystyle=\overline{ag}\cdot\xi

\displaystyle=\psi(a\otimes\overline{g})\xi,

as claimed.

We now apply the construction of the complex
\mathcal{S}^{\bullet}(-)
(taking as input a right
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-module
\mathcal{N}
) to the particular case where
\mathcal{N}:=\mathscr{A}_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{M}
, i.e., we put for all
\ell\in{\mathds{Z}}

\mathcal{C}^{-\ell}(\mathcal{M}):=\mathcal{S}^{-\ell}(\mathscr{A}_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{M}),

yielding a complex
(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet},\delta)
. It is readily checked that since
\mathscr{A}_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{M}
is also a left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-module, the differentials
\delta^{-\ell}
are now left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-linear. Again, it is an easy exercise to see that this construction is functorial, so that
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(-)
yields an exact functor from the category of left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-modules to the category of complexes of left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-modules.

Pursuing the above example where
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}
, we see immediately that

H^{0}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}))\cong\hat{\tau}^{\mathscr{A}}.

We also have the following important homological property of this complex.

Lemma 6.9
.

For any left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-module
\mathcal{M}
,

\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})
is a resolution of
H^{0}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}))
by left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-modules
(which in general are not
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-free though).
In particular, for
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}
, we obtain
that
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}})
is a resolution of
\hat{\tau}^{\mathscr{A}}
by left
\mathscr{A}
-modules.

Proof.

We follow a standard strategy by filtering

\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})
by degree using the natural
filtration on
\mathscr{A}_{V}
. More precisely, using

\mathscr{A}_{V}=\mathcal{O}_{V}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\mathcal{U}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
, we set
F_{k}\mathscr{A}_{V}:=\mathcal{O}_{V}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}F_{k}(\mathcal{U}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
, where
F_{\bullet}(\mathcal{U}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
is the standard filtration on the universal enveloping algebra. By the Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt theorem, we have

\gr_{\bullet}\mathscr{A}_{V}\cong\mathcal{O}_{V}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\textup{Sym}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}).

We consider the induced filtration

F_{\bullet}(\mathscr{A}_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{M})=F_{\bullet}(\mathscr{A}_{V})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{M}
on the left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-module

\mathscr{A}_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{M}
. Then we have the following isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}_{V}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\textup{Sym}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
-modules

\gr_{\bullet}(\mathscr{A}_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{M})\cong\gr_{\bullet}(\mathscr{A}_{V})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{M}\cong\textup{Sym}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\mathcal{M}.

Then we consider the filtration on
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})
defined as

F_{k}\mathcal{C}^{-\ell}(\mathcal{M}):=F_{k+\ell}(\mathscr{A}_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{M})\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\bigwedge^{\ell}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}.

This makes
F_{\bullet}\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})
into a filtered complex, and by the usual arguments one checks that

\gr_{\bullet}^{F}\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})\cong\textup{Kos}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\textup{Sym}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}),(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{\dim(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})})),

for some basis
(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{\dim(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})})
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
. Since clearly

\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{\dim(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})}
is a regular sequence on
\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\textup{Sym}^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime})
, we obtain
H^{i}(\gr_{\bullet}^{F}\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}))=0
for
i<0
. Then by a general argument (see, e.g.
[
SST00
, Theorem 4.3.5]
) it follows that

H^{0}(\gr_{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})))=\gr_{\bullet}H^{0}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})).

We have therefore shown that
\gr^{F}_{\bullet}\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})
is a resolution of

\gr_{\bullet}H^{0}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}))
, but then
the original complex
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}))
is a resolution of
H^{0}(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}))
, which is the first statement of the lemma.
Since, as remarked above, we have

H^{0}\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}})\cong\hat{\tau}^{\mathscr{A}}
,

\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}})
is a resolution of

\hat{\tau}^{\mathscr{A}}

by left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-modules.
∎

The terms of the complex
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})
are not
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-free in general. This is cured by the following construction.

Lemma 6.10
.

There exists a finite resolution
(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M}),d)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{M}
by left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-modules that are free over
\mathcal{O}_{V}
.

Proof.

We first construct via induction an infinite resolution
\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}
of
\mathcal{M}
by left
\mathcal{A}_{V}
-modules that are free (but possibly of infinite rank) over
\mathcal{O}_{V}
.

Let
W^{0}
be the
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
-submodule generated by a global
\mathcal{O}_{V}
-generating set of
\mathcal{M}
. Then
\mathcal{G}^{0}:=\mathcal{O}_{V}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}W^{0}
is a left
\mathcal{A}_{V}
-module via

\displaystyle f\cdot(g\otimes w)

\displaystyle=(fg)\otimes w\qquad(f\in\mathcal{O}_{V}),

\displaystyle\xi\cdot(g\otimes w)

\displaystyle=(Z_{V}(\xi)(g))\otimes w+g\otimes(\xi\cdot w)\qquad(\xi\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}).

The obvious map
\mathcal{G}^{0}\to\mathcal{M}
is surjective and
\mathcal{A}_{V}
-linear. Repeating this procedure with
\ker(\mathcal{G}^{0}\to\mathcal{M})
, and continuing in that way, we get an infinite resolution
\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}
of
\mathcal{M}
of the required type.

We now construct
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})
: Since
\mathcal{O}_{V}
has finite global dimension (say equal to
n
),
\im(\mathcal{G}^{-n}\to\mathcal{G}^{-n+1})
is
\mathcal{O}_{V}
-projective (see, e.g.,
[
Sta22
,
Lemma 00O5
]
) and therefore
\mathcal{O}_{V}
-free. Thus,

\mathcal{F}^{-i}(\mathcal{M}):=\begin{cases}\mathcal{G}^{-i},&\text{if }i<n,\\
\im(\mathcal{G}^{-n}\to\mathcal{G}^{-n+1}),&\text{if }i=n,\\
0,&\text{if }i>n.\end{cases}

with the differential induced from
\mathcal{G}^{\bullet}
works.
∎

Remark 6.11
.

For what follows, a resolution of modules that have possibly infinite rank over
\mathcal{O}_{V}
as just constructed is sufficient. However, it is actually possible to obtain a resolution by finite rank
\mathcal{O}_{V}
-modules under the additional assumption that
\mathcal{M}
is graded,
and finitely generated over
\mathcal{O}_{V}
by homogeneous elements such that the grading is compatible with the left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-structure on
\mathcal{M}
. This is in particular the case for
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}
, which is the only case that we will use below. Namely, under these assumptions, the
\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
-submodule
W^{0}
constructed in each step is then necessarily contained in a finite number of homogeneous components of
\mathcal{M}
, i.e. in a finite dimensional vector space. This suffices to obtain a free
\mathcal{O}_{V}
-module of finite rank
\mathcal{G}^{0}
as above,
which is again graded in a compatible way with the left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-action, and then one argues again by induction.

In the sequel, we specialize to the case
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}
.
According to the previous lemma, by applying the functor

\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(-)
to the
\mathcal{O}_{V}
-free resolution
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}
by left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-modules,
we obtain the double complex

\mathcal{K}^{\bullet,\bullet}:=\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}))

and its associated total complex
\textup{Tot}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{K}^{\bullet,\bullet})
. Then

\textup{Tot}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{K}^{\bullet,\bullet})
provides a resolution of
\hat{\tau}^{\mathscr{A}}
by free left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-modules (of possibly infinite rank). Therefore, we have

\omega_{V}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}\hat{\tau}^{\mathscr{A}}\cong\omega_{\mathscr{A}}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}\textup{Tot}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{K}^{\bullet,\bullet})\cong\textup{Tot}^{\bullet}(\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}\mathcal{K}^{\bullet,\bullet})

Consider the spectral sequence associated to the double complex
\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}\mathcal{K}^{\bullet,\bullet}

with
E_{1}
-term given by first taking vertical cohomology, i.e.

E_{1}^{p,q}:=H^{q}(\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}\mathcal{C}^{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}})))\Longrightarrow H^{p+q}(\omega_{V}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}\hat{\tau}^{\mathscr{A}}).

Then we have the following

Lemma 6.12
.

The above sequence collapses at the
E_{1}
-term, and we have

\omega_{V}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}\hat{\tau}^{\mathscr{A}}\simeq(\mathcal{S}^{\bullet}(\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}),\delta),

where we consider the right module structure on
\mathcal{N}=\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}
coming from the tensor product of the right
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-module
\omega_{X}
with the left
\mathscr{A}_{V}
-module
\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}
.
Explicitly, we have

\mathcal{S}^{\ell}(\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}):=\omega_{V}/\mathcal{I}\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\bigwedge^{-\ell}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},

and where the differentials are

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\delta^{-\ell}\colon\displaystyle\frac{\omega_{V}}{\mathcal{I}\omega_{V}}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\bigwedge^{-\ell}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle\frac{\omega_{V}}{\mathcal{I}\omega_{V}}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\bigwedge^{-\ell+1}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\\
\\
(f\cdot\vol)\otimes(\xi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\xi_{\ell})&\longmapsto&\scriptstyle\sum\limits_{i=1}^{\ell}(-1)^{i-1}(-\Lie_{Z_{V}(\xi_{i})}-\beta^{\prime}(\xi_{i}))(f\cdot\vol)\otimes(\xi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{\xi}_{i}\wedge\ldots\wedge\xi_{\ell})+\\
&&\scriptstyle\sum\limits_{1\leq i<j\leq\ell}(-1)^{i+j}(f\cdot\vol)\otimes([\xi_{i},\xi_{j}]\wedge\xi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{\xi}_{i}\wedge\ldots\wedge\widehat{\xi}_{j}\wedge\ldots\wedge\xi_{\ell})\end{array}

Proof.

According to the above construction, we have

\displaystyle\displaystyle H^{q}(\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}\mathcal{C}^{p}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}})))

\displaystyle\quad=\displaystyle\frac{\displaystyle\ker\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}(\mathscr{A}_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{F}^{q}(\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}))\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\bigwedge^{p}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},\id\otimes\id\otimes d^{q}\otimes\id}}\right)}{\displaystyle\textup{im}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathscr{A}_{V}}(\mathscr{A}_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{F}^{q-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}))\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\bigwedge^{p}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},\id\otimes\id\otimes d^{q-1}\otimes\id}}\right)}

\displaystyle\quad=\displaystyle\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}H^{q}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}))\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\bigwedge^{p}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\quad=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if }q<0,\\
\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\bigwedge^{p}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},&\text{if }q=0.\end{cases}

(recall that
d
is the differential of the complex
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}})
)
from which it is obvious that the spectral sequence collapses, and that the induced differential
\delta^{-\ell}\colon\mathcal{S}^{\ell}(\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}})=E_{1}^{\ell,0}\rightarrow E_{1}^{\ell+1,0}=\mathcal{S}^{\ell+1}(\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}})
is as indicated.
∎

Using all these preliminaries, we finally obtain the vanishing of the two de Rham cohomology groups we are interested in.

Proof of
6.7
.

It remains to show that under the assumptions of the proposition, we have
H^{k}(\mathcal{S}^{\bullet}(\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}))=0
for
k=0,-1
. Let us first notice that the complex
\mathcal{S}^{\bullet}(\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}})

is naturally graded by the grading of
\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}
and of
\mathcal{O}_{V}
(by putting
\deg(\vol):=\dim(V)
) and by setting
\deg(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}):=0
.
Then it is easily verfied that the morphism
Z_{V}
is homogeneous of degree
0
, and therefore also the differentials
\delta^{-\ell}
are so. Consequently, it suffices to calculate the cohomology of the graded parts of
\mathcal{S}^{\bullet}(\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}})
.

The relevant maps in this complex are as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\delta^{-1}\colon\frac{\omega_{V}}{\mathcal{I}\omega_{V}}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle\frac{\omega_{V}}{\mathcal{I}\omega_{V}}\\
\\
(f\cdot\vol)\otimes\xi&\longmapsto&(-\Lie_{Z_{V}(\xi)}-\beta^{\prime}(\xi))(f\cdot\vol)\\
\\
\\
\\
\displaystyle\delta^{-2}\colon\frac{\omega_{V}}{\mathcal{I}\omega_{V}}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\bigwedge^{2}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}&\longrightarrow&\displaystyle\frac{\omega_{V}}{\mathcal{I}\omega_{V}}\otimes_{\mathds{C}}\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\\
\\
(f\cdot\vol)\otimes\vartheta\wedge\eta&\longmapsto&(-\Lie_{Z_{V}(\vartheta)}-\beta^{\prime}(\vartheta))(f\cdot\vol)\otimes\eta+(\Lie_{Z_{V}(\eta)}+\beta^{\prime}(\eta))(f\cdot\vol)\otimes\vartheta\\
&&-(f\cdot\vol)\otimes[\vartheta,\eta]\\
\\
&&\displaystyle=\delta^{-1}((f\cdot\vol)\otimes\vartheta)\otimes\eta-\delta^{-1}((f\cdot\vol)\otimes\eta)\otimes\vartheta-(f\cdot\vol)\otimes[\vartheta,\eta].\end{array}

In order to describe these morphisms, first notice that for any
\theta\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
, we have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\Lie_{Z_{V}(\theta)}(\vol)&=&\displaystyle\Lie_{-\sum_{i,j}d\rho(\theta)_{ji}x_{i}\partial_{x_{j}}}(\vol)=-\sum_{i,j}d\rho(\theta)_{ji}\Lie_{x_{i}\partial_{x_{j}}}(\vol)\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle-\sum_{i,j}d\rho(\theta)_{ji}\delta_{ij}\cdot\vol=-\textup{trace}(d\rho(\theta))\cdot\vol.\end{array}

We thus get

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Lie_{Z_{V}(\theta)}+\beta^{\prime}(\theta)}}\right)(f\cdot\vol)&=&\big(Z_{V}(\theta)(f)-f\cdot\textup{trace}(d\rho(\theta))+\underbrace{\beta^{\prime}(\theta)}_{=\textup{trace}(d\rho(\theta))-\beta(\theta)}\cdot f\big)\cdot\vol\\
\\
&=&\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z_{V}(\theta)(f)-\beta(\theta)f}}\right)\cdot\vol\end{array}

(24)

After these preliminaries, let us first show that
H^{0}(\mathcal{S}^{\bullet}(\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}))=0
, i.e., that the the morphism
\delta^{-1}
is surjective.
According to Lemma 4.3. of our paper, for
\xi=\mathbf{e}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
, we have

Z_{V}(\mathbf{e})=-E:=-\sum_{i=1}^{\dim V}x_{i}\partial_{x_{i}},

when
x_{1},\ldots,x_{\dim(V)}
are coordinates on
V
.
We thus have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\displaystyle\delta^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((f\cdot\vol)\otimes\mathbf{e}}}\right)&=&\displaystyle(-\Lie_{Z_{V}(\mathbf{e})}-\beta^{\prime}(\mathbf{e}))(f\cdot\vol)=(E(f)+\beta(\mathbf{e})f)\cdot\vol\end{array}

Since
E(f)=d\cdot f
for
f
homogeneous of (non-negative) degree
d
, the fact that
\beta(\mathbf{e})\notin{\mathds{Z}}_{\leq 0}
shows that
\delta^{-1}
is surjective, hence
H^{0}(\mathcal{S}^{\bullet}(\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}))=0
.

The vanishing of
H^{-1}(\mathcal{S}^{\bullet}(\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}))
will similarly be shown in each degree of the complex. Therefore, suppose that we have homogeneous elements
f_{i}\in\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}
all of which have the same degree
d\in{\mathds{Z}}_{\geq 0}
and
\xi_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}
for
i=1,\ldots,r
such that

\delta^{-1}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{r}(f_{i}\cdot\vol)\otimes\xi_{i}\bigg)=0.

By assumption, we have
d+\beta(\mathbf{e})\neq 0
. Then it follows (using
[\mathbf{e},\xi_{i}]=0
) that

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\delta^{-2}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\frac{f_{i}}{d+\beta(\mathbf{e})}\cdot\vol)\otimes\mathbf{e}\wedge\xi_{i}\bigg)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\delta^{-2}\bigg(\frac{f_{i}}{d+\beta(\mathbf{e})}\cdot\vol\otimes\mathbf{e}\wedge\xi_{i}\bigg)\\
\\
=\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}\bigg(\delta^{-1}\big((\frac{f_{i}}{d+\beta(\mathbf{e})}\cdot\vol)\otimes\mathbf{e}\big)\otimes\xi_{i}\bigg)-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\delta^{-1}\bigg((\frac{f_{i}}{d+\beta(\mathbf{e})}\cdot\vol)\otimes\xi_{i}\bigg)\otimes\mathbf{e}\\
\\
=\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}\bigg(\delta^{-1}\big((\frac{f_{i}}{d+\beta(\mathbf{e})}\cdot\vol)\otimes\mathbf{e}\big)\otimes\xi_{i}\bigg)-\frac{1}{d+\beta(\mathbf{e})}\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{r}\delta^{-1}\bigg((f_{i}\cdot\vol)\otimes\xi_{i}\bigg)}_{=0}\otimes\mathbf{e}\\
\\
=\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}\bigg(\frac{E(f_{i})+\beta(\mathbf{e})f_{i}}{d+\beta(\mathbf{e})}\cdot\vol\otimes\xi_{i}\bigg),\end{array}

so that
\sum_{i=1}^{r}(f_{i}\cdot\vol)\otimes\xi_{i}\in\textup{im}(\delta^{-2})
, thus showing
H^{-1}(\mathcal{S}^{\bullet}(\omega_{V}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{V}}\mathcal{O}_{\hat{X}}))=0
.
∎

The next statement summarizes the results obtained so far in
Section

6
. We consider the situation as described before
4.34
and we would like to describe how the
\mathscr{D}_{V}
-module
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
is related to its restriction to
V\setminus\{0\}
.
We only state the results under the simplifying assumption that
G
is semi-simple, since this is the main case of interest and since it allows us to use the results proved in
Section

5
.
Recall that under the assumption that
G
(and cosequently its Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
) is semi-simple, we necessarily have
\beta_{|\mathfrak{g}}=0
.

Corollary 6.13
.

In the above situation, assume that
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\neq 0
. Then we have:

1.

\beta(\mathbf{e})\in{\mathds{Q}}_{\geq 0}
.

2.

If
\beta(\mathbf{e})=0
, then
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
is a free
\mathcal{O}_{W}
-module of finite positive rank.

3.

If
\beta(\mathbf{e})\in{\mathds{Q}}_{>0}
, then
we have an isomorphism in
\textup{Mod}_{h}(\mathscr{D}_{V})

H^{0}j_{\dagger}j^{+}\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)

4.

If
\beta(\mathbf{e})\in{\mathds{Q}}_{>0}\setminus{\mathds{Z}}_{>0}
, then
we have isomorphisms in
\textup{Mod}_{h}(\mathscr{D}_{V})

\begin{array}[]{rcl}j_{+}j^{+}\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\longrightarrow}}&\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta),\\
\\
&\textup{and}\\
\\
j_{+}j^{+}\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)&\cong&j_{\dagger}j^{+}\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\end{array}

in particular, we have
H^{i}(j_{\star}j^{+}\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta))=0
for
i\neq 0
and for
\star\in\{+,{\dagger}\}
in this case. Furthermore,
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
is simple.

Proof.

1.

This is exactly the statement of
5.2
.

2.

It has been shown in
5.14
that
X
is Fano. This implies by
4.34
that if
\beta(\mathbf{e})=0
, then necessarily
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)_{|V\backslash\{0\}}=0
. Therefore,
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},0)
has support in the origin in
V
and consequently,
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},0)
is a free
\mathcal{O}_{W}
-module (of positive rank by the assumption
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\neq 0
). Notice that the non-vanishing of
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\neq 0
is automatic if
\beta(\mathbf{e})=0
: in this case, any constant function on
W
is a (classical) solution to
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},0)
, since it is annihilated by any operator in the denominator.

3.

This follows directly from
6.3
.

4.

The isomorphism

j_{+}j^{+}\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\cong\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
is exactly the content of
6.2
(applying it for
Y=\hat{X}\setminus\{0\}
and
\overline{Y}=\hat{X}
). Moreover, the second isomorphism is obviously true if
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)=0
. Otherwise, we must have by the first isomorphism that

j^{+}\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\neq 0
, but then by

4.34
we know that

j^{+}\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\cong i^{\prime}_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k}

with
\beta(\mathbf{e})=\ell/k
. Since
i^{\prime}
is proper, we are therefore left to show that

j_{+}i^{\prime}_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k}\cong j_{\dagger}i^{\prime}_{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k},

(25)

but this follows from the proof of
3.8
, points 1. and 3., by noticing that we have
\hat{\iota}=j\circ i^{\prime}\circ\textup{inv}^{-1}\colon L^{\vee,*}\hookrightarrow V
. It follows from Eq. (
25
) that
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
is an intermediate extension of
i^{\prime}_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k}
. Since
i^{\prime}_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k}
is simple, we conclude that
\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
is simple as well. Since
\FL^{V}
is an equivalence of categories the claim follows.

∎

6.3
Tautological systems as mixed Hodge modules

The purpose of this section is to finally achieve the functorial construction of tautological systems
announced in the introduction (more specifically, in
1.2
), by combining the results in

Section

3.3
, the description of
\hat{\tau}_{|V\setminus\{0\}}
from
4.34
as well as the localization resp. colocalization properties of
\hat{\tau}
summarized in
6.13
above.

Let us recall once again the setup we are working with: We let
X
be a projective variety and we consider a transitive action of a reductive connected algebraic group
G
on
X
.
We let
L\rightarrow X
be a very ample
G
-equivariant line bundle. We extend the group action on
X
and
L
to a an action of
G^{\prime}:=\mathds{C}^{*}\times G
by letting the
\mathds{C}^{*}
-factor act trivially on
X
and by inverse scaling in the fibers of
L
.
We consider the
G^{\prime}
-representation
V:=H^{0}(X,\mathscr{L})^{\vee}
and the equivariant closed embedding
X\hookrightarrow\mathds{P}V
defined by
|\mathscr{L}|
.
Let
\hat{X}\subseteq V
be the affine cone of
X
in
V
, and we have an isomorphism
\hat{X}\setminus\{0\}\cong L^{\vee,*}
by identifying
L^{\vee}
with the blow-up of
\hat{X}
at the origin. We write
\hat{\iota}\colon L^{\vee,*}\cong\hat{X}\setminus\{0\}\to V
for the locally closed embedding given as the composition of
the closed embedding
i\colon\hat{X}\setminus\{0\}\hookrightarrow V\setminus\{0\}
with the canonical open embedding

j\colon V\setminus\{0\}\hookrightarrow V
.
Together with the isomorphism
\textup{inv}\colon L^{*}\to L^{\vee,*}
given by inverting fibers, we obtain a locally closed embedding
\iota\colon L^{*}\hookrightarrow V
defined by
\iota:=\hat{\iota}\circ\textup{inv}
.

For the convenience of the reader, we summarize the various maps that occur by extending the diagram (
14
) from
Section

4.4
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{X}\setminus\{0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\setminus\{0\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textup{Bl}_{\{0\}}(\hat{X})\cong L^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\vee,*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{L}}
inv
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{L^{\vee}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\iota}}

(26)

We let, as before,

\beta\colon\mathfrak{g}^{\prime}\to\mathds{C}
be a Lie algebra homomorphism satisfying
{\beta_{|{\mathfrak{g}}}}\equiv 0
. Denote
W:=V^{\vee}
.

Theorem 6.14
.

Under the above hypotheses, the following statements hold.

1.

Assume that

\beta(\mathbf{e})=\ell/k\in\mathds{Q}\setminus\mathds{Z}
. The tautological system
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
is non-zero if and only if
\mathscr{L}^{\otimes\ell}\cong\omega_{X}^{\otimes(-k)}
as
G
-equivariant line bundles. In this case, we have
isomorphisms

\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\cong\FL^{V}(\iota_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k})\cong\FL^{V}(\iota_{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k})

in
\textup{Mod}(\mathscr{D}_{W})
, and the
\mathscr{D}_{W}
-module
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
underlies a complex pure Hodge module on
W
of weight
\dim(X)+\dim(W)
. Moreover,
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
is simple, and, consequently, the local system associated to

\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)_{|W\backslash\textup{Sing}(\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta))}
is irreducible.

2.

If
\beta(\mathbf{e})\in{\mathds{Z}}_{>0}
,
then
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
is non-zero if and only if
\mathscr{L}^{\otimes\beta(\mathbf{e})}\cong\omega_{X}^{\vee}
as
G
-equivariant line bundles, in which case we have an isomorphism

\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\cong\FL^{V}(H^{0}\iota_{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}})

in
\textup{Mod}(\mathscr{D}_{W})
. Then the
\mathscr{D}_{W}
-module
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
underlies an element of
\textup{MHM}(W)
with weights in
\{\dim(W)+\dim(X),\,\dim(W)+\dim(X)+1\}
.

Proof.

Under the assumptions of the theorem, we have in both cases that

j^{+}\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\cong\begin{cases}i^{\prime}_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k}&\text{if }\mathscr{L}^{\otimes\ell}\cong\omega_{X}^{\otimes(-k)},\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

by
4.34
(with
\ell=\beta(\mathbf{e})
,
k=1
in case
2
), notice that we had implicitly identified
L^{*}
with
\hat{X}\setminus\{0\}
in
4.34
.

We now distinguish the two cases.

1.

Since

\ell/k\notin{\mathds{Z}}
, we know from

6.2

that

\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\cong j_{+}j^{+}\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta).

Therefore, since
\iota=j\circ i^{\prime}
, we conclude that

\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\cong\begin{cases}\iota_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k}&\text{if }\mathscr{L}^{\otimes\ell}\cong\omega_{X}^{\otimes(-k)},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

As we have
\FL^{V}(\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta))=\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
by
1.1
, we obtain

\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\cong\begin{cases}\FL^{V}(\iota_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k})&\text{if }\mathscr{L}^{\otimes\ell}\cong\omega_{X}^{\otimes(-k)},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

as required. The fact that

\FL^{V}(\iota_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k})\cong\FL^{V}(\iota_{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k})
is simply the
\mathscr{D}
-module version of
3.8
, point 3. Then it follows as in
6.13
that
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
is a simple
\mathscr{D}_{W}
-module, and in particular that the local system (and the monodromy representation) of its restriction to its smooth part is irreducible.

For the second statement, assume
\mathscr{L}^{\otimes\ell}\cong\omega_{X}^{\otimes(-k)}
and recall from

3.8
that

\FL^{V}(\hat{\iota}_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{-\ell/k})\cong a_{W,+}ev^{\dagger}j_{L,{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k}

as elements in
D^{b}_{h}(\mathscr{D}_{W})
, using the notations from
3.8
. Since we have
\textup{inv}_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}}^{-\ell/k}\cong\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k}
, we get

\FL^{V}(\iota_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k})\cong a_{W,+}ev^{\dagger}j_{L,{\dagger}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k}

in
D^{b}_{h}(\mathscr{D}_{W})
. However, as we have just proved, this is actually a single degree complex isomorphic to the tautological system
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
. Hence it follows from the second statement of
3.8
that this
\mathscr{D}_{W}
-module underlies the pure complex Hodge module

{{}^{H}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\ell/k}=H^{0}({{}^{H}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}^{\ell/k})=a_{W,*}ev^{*}j_{L,!}{{}^{H}\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{L^{*}}^{\ell/k}[\dim W-1]

which has weight
\dim(X)+\dim(W)
.

2.

Since
\beta(\mathbf{e})\notin\mathds{Z}_{\leq 0}
, we know from
6.3
that

\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\cong\begin{cases}H^{0}j_{\dagger}j^{+}\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)&\text{if }\mathscr{L}^{\otimes\beta(\mathbf{e})}\cong\omega_{X}^{\vee},\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Hence,

\hat{\tau}(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\cong\begin{cases}H^{0}j_{\dagger}i^{\prime}_{+}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}&\text{if }\mathscr{L}^{\otimes\beta(\mathbf{e})}\cong\omega_{X}^{\vee},\\
0&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

using
\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{\beta(\mathbf{e})}\cong\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}
since
\beta(\mathbf{e})\in\mathds{Z}
. Since
i^{\prime}
is a closed embedding, we have
i^{\prime}_{+}\cong i^{\prime}_{\dagger}
, so, using
\iota=j\circ i^{\prime}
, we conclude the first statement.

The second statement then follows again from
3.8
, points 1. and 2. More precisely, we had shown there that
\FL^{V}(\hat{\iota}_{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}})
underlies
{{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L}\in\textup{MHM}(W)
, so that

\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\cong\FL^{V}(H^{0}\iota_{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}})\cong\FL^{V}(H^{0}\hat{\iota}_{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}})\cong H^{0}\FL^{V}(\hat{\iota}_{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{L^{\vee,*}})

underlies
H^{0}({{}^{H,!}\!\!}\mathcal{M}_{L})\in\textup{MHM}(W)
. The weight estimate then follows directly from
3.8
, point 4. for the case
k=0
. ∎

As a corollary, we solve the holonomic rank problem from
[
BHL+14
, Conjecture 1.3.]
in general (i.e. for all homogeneous spaces and all possible equivariant line bundles that give rise to non-zero tautological systems). Recall from the discussion before
3.9
that
\mathcal{U}:=(W\times X)\setminus ev^{-1}(0)\subseteq W\times X
and that
a_{\mathcal{U}}\colon\mathcal{U}\rightarrow W
denotes the restriction of the first projection
a_{W}\colon W\times X\rightarrow W
.
Moreover, for any
\lambda\in W
, we write
i_{\lambda}\colon\{\lambda\}\hookrightarrow W
for the corresponding closed embedding, we let
U_{\lambda}\subset X
be the complement of the zero locus of the section
\lambda\colon X\rightarrow L
, and we denote by
\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{\lambda}
the complex local system on
U_{\lambda}
that underlies the pure complex Hodge module
\lambda^{*}_{|U_{\lambda}}\,{}^{H\!}\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{\beta}_{L^{*}}[-1]
.

Corollary 6.15
.

1.

Under the assumptions of
6.14
, point 1., i.e.,
\beta(\mathbf{e})=\ell/k\in{\mathds{Q}}\setminus{\mathds{Z}}
and
\mathscr{L}^{\otimes\ell}\cong\omega_{X}^{\otimes(-k)}
as
G
-equivariant line bundles, we have isomorphisms in
\textup{Mod}_{h}(\mathscr{D}_{W})

\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\cong a_{\mathcal{U},{\dagger}}{ev_{|\mathcal{U}}^{+}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{-\ell/k}\cong a_{\mathcal{U},+}{ev_{|\mathcal{U}}^{+}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}^{-\ell/k}.

As a consequence, we have an isomorphism of vector spaces

H^{m}(i_{\lambda}^{+}\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta))\cong H^{\dim(X)+m}(U_{\lambda},\,\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\ell/k}_{\lambda})

(27)

resp.

H^{m}(i_{\lambda}^{\dagger}\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta))\cong H_{c}^{\dim(X)+m}(U_{\lambda},\,\underline{{\mathds{C}}}^{-\ell/k}_{\lambda})

(28)

for all
m\in\mathds{Z}
and for all
\lambda\in W
.

2.

If we assume that the hypotheses of
6.14
, point 2., hold true (i.e.,
\beta(\mathbf{e})\in{\mathds{Z}}_{>0}
and
\mathscr{L}^{\otimes\beta(\mathbf{e})}\cong\omega_{X}^{\vee}
as
G
-equivariant line bundles), then we have an isomorphism

\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\cong H^{0}a_{\mathcal{U},+}{ev_{|\mathcal{U}}^{+}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}.

In particular, we obtain for all
\lambda\in W
an isomorphism

H^{0}(i_{\lambda}^{+}\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta))\cong H^{\dim(X)}(U_{\lambda},\,{\mathds{C}}).

(29)

3.

The holonomic rank of
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
is given in the two cases as

\dim H_{c}^{\dim(X)}(U_{\lambda},\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{\lambda}^{-\ell/k})\simeq\dim H^{\dim(X)}(U_{\lambda},\underline{{\mathds{C}}}_{\lambda}^{-\ell/k})\quad\quad\quad\textup{ if }\beta(\mathbf{e})\in{\mathds{Q}}\setminus{\mathds{Z}},

resp.

\dim H^{\dim(X)}(U_{\lambda},{\mathds{C}})\quad\quad\quad\textup{ if }\beta(\mathbf{e})\in{\mathds{Z}}_{>0},

for any value
\lambda\in W
that lies outside the singular locus of
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
.

Notice that
H^{0}(i_{\lambda}^{+}\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta))
is the space dual to the space of (classical) solution of
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
at the point
\lambda
, so that that
Eq.

27
and
Eq.

29
also comprise and generalize
[
HLZ16
, Corollary 2.3]
.

Proof.

1.

Using the previous
6.14
, the first statement is exactly the
\mathscr{D}
-module version of
3.9
, 1. Similarly,
the second statement follows from
3.9
, 2.

2.

The first statement is obtained by combining
6.14
with
3.9
, 1. In order to get the second one, we apply the functor
H^{0}i_{\lambda}^{+}
to the isomorphism
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)\cong H^{0}a_{\mathcal{U},+}{ev_{|\mathcal{U}}^{+}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}
. This shows that
H^{0}i_{\lambda}^{+}\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
sits at the origin of the
E_{2}
-term of the (third quadrant) Grothendieck spectral sequence for the composition of the functors
i_{\lambda}^{+}
and
a_{\mathcal{U},+}
. Therefore, it is isomorphic to the
(0,0)
-spot of the abutement, which is

H^{0}i_{\lambda}^{+}a_{\mathcal{U},+}{ev_{|\mathcal{U}}^{+}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}\cong H^{0}a_{\mathcal{U},+}{ev_{|\mathcal{U}}^{+}}\mathcal{O}_{L^{*}}.

3.

This follows from point 1. resp. 2. since the holonomic rank is the fibre dimension
of
\tau(\rho,\hat{X},\beta)
at any
\lambda\in W
outside the singular locus. For such points
\lambda
we also have
i_{\lambda}^{+}=i_{\lambda}^{\dagger}
and this is then an exact functor.

∎
